the smallest possible number of Her Majesty's subjects in the United Kingdom will be entitled to a vote. Inclnl- ing our soldiers, now serving us with such splendid valour in South Africa, at least one in five is deprived of his constitutional right to pass judgment upon the acts of the Government, out of the number who would have enjoyed that right had the Election been post- poned until January. That is to say, the Government thus disfranchises something like ten times as many as all the adult British males who worked in the Transvaal added together. By January. as

I an happy to believe, the large majority ol our soldiers

will have returned home, and assuredly no others are so well entitled to be beard as they. They will have much to say about the management of the war. Under these circumstandes, and especially looking to the fact that no General Election has been held in the autumn for nearly a hundred years. exc3pt upon a specially peepared new register. I consider the demand of the Government for an immediate appeal to the people to be no better than a solemn sham; and I confidently ask you to condemn it accordingly. Mr. Chamberlain says in his Address to the Electors o! West Birmingham, that we are now asked to say wh3ther or not the war was just and inevitahle, and that their opponents accuse the Government of deliberately pro- voking a war for which they made no prepsration.l know of no Liberal in a responsible position who has thus accused th 3 Government. But passing that by, I declare my opinion on the contrary to be that the Government never intented to go to war, sail that they themselves, mislead by the assurances of Mr. Cecil Rhodes and his friends, had no ilea that a war was in- evitable ; for how otherwise are we to account for the notorious fact that they were so ill pre- pared for it, in spite of having spent, last year, no less than twelve millions of money upon our Army and Navy more than the 'late Liberal Government spent in their last year of office. I have never said, and do not say now, that the war was unjust, for the 'reason that it was we who were first attacked. We were bound to fight and to win. These are my answers to Mr. Chamberlain.

At the same time I blame the Government for having knowingly allowed the Transvaal f3r three years to con- tinue, unhindered, to accumulate enormous armaments— armaments which could only have been wanted for use against us ; and I consider Mr. Balfour's plea that our hands were tied by the Raid" to be an utterly inadequate excuse. This lack of moral courage on the part of the Government has cost us millions on millions of hard-

earned money, and has brought bitter grief into thousands of humble homes.

I blame the Government far having. at the outset of the war. refused Australia's offer of m ounted troops, and for having replied" Unmounted men preferred," This was no trivial and pardonable slip, but a huge and terrible blunder, which, coupled with the inexcusable delay in sending out cavalry from home, resulted in diens ter. upon disas'er to our troops during the sad moat he of last autumn and winter, a blunderfor which alone this Government deserves t be driven frora oftlle• Having myself, like so many of us, near relatives serving in South Africa, I bitterly re- sent those disasters, and I hold the Government. and not our soldiers, resoolsible tor them.

The settlement praposel with rear d to tha Transvaal and the Orange Rivar Ojirnv by Lot tlisbiry,Mr. Balfour. and qr. Chamberltin, is agree I to by Lori Rosebery, Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerol tn, Sir Wiliam Harcourt. and Hr. Morley ; so toat upon this question, which the Tory Party are endea vouring to make the test qaestion at this Illeetion. th ere is s‘bsolutely no differeqce between the two great parties In the State. With this proposal so made and so agreed to, I am in complete amord.

As heretofore. I appeal to you as a thorough going Liberal. steadily holding to the generous side in politics. I desire a Radical reform in our anticulated Land Laws, convinced tang years ago by Richard Cobden that the party who bAnzi a'io It, free trade in lind will do even more for our country than that which secured free trade in corn.

I desire that the people should have the power now vested in the magistrates with regard to the existence of public houses in their immediate neighbourhood. Those who use the pusli houses. and those who suffer from the public house., have a o lear moral right to thls power. No man should be a licensed victualler who did not Bell victuals; and no victnaller should be tied to a brewer. I desire to ame n d our iniquitous system of local rating by secur jug that all rateable property shall pay according t its real altosl n 2 trket value. All) attempts to deal with the pressin4 criestion of overcrowding in towns, without this change in the law, will be a mockery. I desire that the pe )ple, now pav,ng at least f mir fifths of t h e cost of our Elementary Schools, should have the full control of those schools, lam convinced that with regard to religious diff ere noes they would be thoroughly fair and considerate toward s each other.

I strongly desire the amendmen tof the Workmen's Campmsatio i Act, so as t [nein le all workmen, and to provide fair 03 11P 313 3:111 hl ill et see of accident, points on which the present Act is ridiculously inadequate. Fair compensation has been paid for all a scitents by my firm since January, 1831, nearly twenty years. We have not waited for the Tories to fulfil their hypocritical promise to grant Old Age Pensions. Our men are the steadiest set of workers in this kingdom—they grow old in our employ, and when they ra tire, we grant pensions in all necessitous eases, depending upon their length of service. My opponents continue to repeat the f set slander that we refuse to take on men over thirty ye trs of size ; we lake on men over thirty years of age nearly every week In the year. Generous mindet men will resent this method of attsck.

I hope to devote the rest of my wlrking life to yoer service; but if any voter in the constituency honestly belleva.s that my opponent has been, or is, or is likely to be, a better friend to England. a batter friend to Ches- hire, or a better frieni te you, than I have been, am, and intend tc be, he is, as far as I am concerned, welcome to express that belief, awl to back it at the ballot box.

Your fathfut servant. JOHN T. BRUNNER.

A -FABLE.—A wine merchant left a suspected assistant in his cellar, and said to him, "Now, lest you should drink the wine while I am away, I will chalk your mouth, so that I may know it." He then rubbed his nail across the man's lips, and pretented to leave the mark of chalk on them. The man drank of the wine, and chalked his mouth, and thus discovered himself. McDOUGAL I A'S PATENT SELF-RAISING FLOUR ZOOM. THE EXAMINER, FRI DAY, SEPTEMBER 28 1900 LADIES' .LETTER

THE APPLETON SHOOTING CASE

The Sentence.

At the Daresbury Petty Sessions on Tuesday, before Mr. J. C. Parr and other magistrates, James Gregson, a 1 teamsroan lately employed on Mr. Yarweod's Farm, 1 Appleton, was charged with unlawfully assaulting Collin Bennett on September 4th, and Collin Bennet was charged with assaulting James Gregson on September 2nd Mr. Jenkins appeared for Gregeon and pleaded not guilty. Superintendent Pearson stated that the prisoner Greg- son was formerly charged with shooting with intent to do grievous bodily harm. Since then he had been in communication with the Chief Constable and he had been authorised by him to reduce the charge to one of common assault. In his opinion he thought that it was a case which called for a severe penalty. Collin Bennett, aged 13, of Gaskell-street, Stockton Heath, said that on the evening of September 4th he went to Mr Yarwood's farm to help the milk bey, Bertram Howells. Howells, while on the farm, took Gregson's broom to brush the cart cut, and when he took it back he met Gregson, who reproached him for using the broom and struck him twic., on the legs. He (witness) and otber boys called Gregson a " coward " whereupon Gregson ran after them twice and failin4 to overtake them brought a gun and tired at them. Witness was shot in the leg and a shot grazed his teoth. He then gave information to the police.—By Mr Jenkins : They did not annoy prisoner at all, they did not uqi bad lan- guage towards him or tease him in any way, except call- ing him a /coward. When prisoner fired, he fired at ' them. _ _

Bertram Howells also gave evidence. Martin Maloney said he saw prisoner put two car- tridges in the gun. He (prisoner) was under the influ- ence of drink at the time. He spoke about the boys annoying him and said he would " shift " them.--By Mr. Jenkins: Prisoner had been aggravated by the boys. He himself was very often teased by the boys, but be took no not's°. _ . .

Sergeant Bourne deposed to arresting the prisoner who, in answer to the charge, said " I fired the gun,but shot into the air." He examined Bennett and the wound had every appearance of being a gun shot wound. Defendant, sworn, admitted that on the evening in question he had had some drink and was nearly drunk. When Howells brought his brush to the stable he threw it on the floor. That vexed him and he struck him with the brush. Bennett and the others Wed him names and he chased them,but failing to catch them determined to frighten them by firing the gun. He fired into the air. They had previously thrown stones at him. On Sunday, September 2nd, he was on London Bridge. Stockton Heath, when he met Bennett, Howells, and others. They annoyed him and in fun he hit them with a thin stick. Bennett threw a stone at him and injured his thigh. Mrs. Savage, Gaskell-street,said she saw the gun firea into the air. .

Mr. Jenkins contended that the boys had annoyed the prisoner, who in a fic of temper brought the gun up on the scene and fired into the air to frighten them. They being frightened ran off and probably Bennett had fallen over some brambles and pricked his leg. Prisoner was rffended at the very least thing. The Chairman, after consulting his colleagues, said prisoner would be seat to gaol for a month with hard labour, the Bench being satisficd that an assault had been committed. The case against Bennett was die. missed.

CHAPTER XXXII.

MAN PROPOSES, GOD DISPOSES,

Duncan Rutland did not sleep throughout the whole night after Ritzoom had left him. He could not. He was again in the toils of a great tempta- tion. 2lle had thought when lie had determined to leave Rome a few weeks before that he had fought his battle and won it, now, however, he realised that it was harder than ever. Then he did not know that Alizon loved him, his hopes of winning her were very dim, now all was different, and that difference made the struggle more painful. The cup of happiness had been lifted to his lips, he had tasted of its joys, and to have it dashed from him for ever seemed more than he could bear. What would life be to him without her? How could he bear to spend perhaps fifty years without having her by his side, knowing all the time that ibe longed to be there? He considered the whole situation again and again. He reviewed every possibility, and it seemed as though there were no alternatives save those which Ritzoom had set before him.

Hour after hour passed, and still he was under the Jesuit's influence, the cruel logic of his words constantly appeared before him, undermining his will and his power of resistance. For inclination, pleasure, aye, the happiness of a lifetime, lay in obedience to the priest's words. Oh, it seemed so easy, so pleasant to yield, to swim with the tide, to live for love! It was so hard, so terribly hard to follow that path which in spite of all his reasonings, duty marked out for him! When morning came the question was still unsettled. He rose as soon as it was light, and walked across the great wild moors which stretched away behind the house in which he lived. Again he prayed for strength as he had prayed before; again he poured out his soul in wild cries to his Creator.

But the questions of his heart were not answered. In the afternoon he went through the town. How glad the people seemed to see him, to shake him by the hand, to speak a kindly word. Many of them were rough in speech, some a trifle coarse, but they were kind at heart, kind and simple. He remembered the words which were spoken on the previous evening, and •at the meeting on the Saturday. He thought of the expressions of their loyalty and their love. He saw again the great throngs of people who had crowded the building. Ah, he might become a greater man by being a barrister, he might occupy a more enviable position from the wordling's standpoint, but would he be a better man in himself ? Did not Ritzoom's suggestion mean sacrificing his manhood? At half-past five the machinery of the mills ceased, and thousands of weavers thronged the streets. Scores of them, aye, hundreds, gave him a cheerful nod and a kind smile; some stopped him and told him how glad they were to see him back again in their midst. Presently he saw old Matthy Bray and Jonas Dixon coming towards him. "Aye, Maaster Rutland," they said, "aw'm reight glad to see tha abaat agean. Yo're lookin' rare and brown, too. Tha ruun tak' care o thysen naa. We can noan afford for thee to laive us and go off to Rome. Tha mun stick to us naa." "Do you really wish that I should? " asked the young man, with a wan smile. "Wish," said old Jonas, "wish, lad? We needn't tell thee that. Saturday neight tould tha that, yi, and yesterday too. Us Lancashire folk are rough and ready; we say straight things, but we know when we like onybody. Aye, 't would be a blow for Tudor, aye, and for Lynford, too, if than wart obliged to laive us. But yo' wil'n't, mester, yo're better naa, aren't you ? " and the old weaver looked into the young man's eyes with a look of affection.

"Oh, yes, I'm much better; but not over strong yet!" "No," said Matthy Bray, "tha wants some good Lancashire food to nit. Yo've 'ad a lot o' Italian rubbish, an' it's noan good for a civilised man. Yo' mun geet plenty of good Lancashire beef, and crairo, and that sort o' thing. Things wi 'a bett o' strengthenin' stuff in "An yo' mun noan work too 'ard. Tak' it aisy for a bit, lad, tak' it aisy. What we want is that Out cud geet wed. A nice Lancashire lase, wi' a bit o' brass. Ther's plenty i' Tudor, Mester Rutland, nice lasses an' oal. Geet wed and then thoult geet settled. Aye, but tha mun settle daan for life, lad. Tha mun noan laive Tudor, we're suited, and thairt suited. Good-neight, mester. " The two weavers walked on, and presently he met more. It was the same everywhere. Rich and poor met him with smiles, and all gave him a word of welcome. _ _

He felt himself to be mean and ungrateful. These people bad showered kindness upon him, and yet he was contemplating leaving them. And yet— well, what should he do? Days passed away, and still be came no nearer a decision. He had not seen Alizon, neither had be heard from her. He hungered for a message, and yet he dared not go near the Priory. He dared not go to places where he might possibly see her.

On the Thursday night, Ritzoom paid another visit to his lodgings. He evidently came in the expectation of receiving Rutland's answer, but in this he was disappointed. The young man was still fighting his battle, a battle between love and duty, between pleasure and pain, and try as he might, he could not make up his mind. "Of course, you—you have seen no one from the Priory since your return to Lynford ? " he said, casnatly, as he rose to take his leave.

"I thought not," and then he looked at Rutland like a skilled mesmerist looks at his victim.

"Thera is nothing wrong at the Priory, I hope?" said the young man, thrown off his guard for a moment.

"There are wet eyes there." "Wet eyes! What do you mean ? " "Surely you can guess," said the Jesuit, as he left the house. "Good-night." All through the night he thought of Alizon, and the memory of the priest's words haunted him. He interpreted them in a hundred ways, and every one of his interpretations made him long to yield to his heart's desire. What should he do? What should he do? The question haunted him throughout the next day, and all the while he felt himself becoming weaker and weaker. At nine o'clock that night he found himself alone fighting the same battle. He had just returned from a meeting, and was now sitting in his lonely rooms. Oh, it was very, very weary, this continuous doubt, this continuous struggle! He could not bear it much longer. He felt sometimes as though he must yield to the conditions which Lady Neville had authorised the priest to make. Presently he drew a chair to his study-desk, the desk which had been presented to him the previous Saturday evening. Yes, he would write that night and decide the question for ever. He took up a pen and wrote the date, then followed the words: "Dear Lady Neville -" So far he got, and then he stopped. Could he resign Tudor Church, for that was what it meant, on the very desk the people had given him a few days !Wore ? On the other hand, could he give up Alizon for ever? He took out his watch and looked at the time. It was the watch the church had given him. He looked around • the room, and saw a number of books which told him of his duty. Oh, but he

could not give up Alizon, and yet-- He heard a knock at the door. No one answered it. Evidently his landlady had gone for a few minutes' chat with a neighbour. It was repeated.

He went to the door, and on opening i, saw a veiled figure, which he could not recognise in the darkness.

"Mrs. Nutter has gone out, I think," he said, "but I daresay she will be back in a few minutes." "May I come in ? " Without a word he closed the door behind her and drew her into the room. "Alizonl" he cried.

She took off her hat and veil and placed them on a chair, and then looked at him like one wondering. "I have heard everything," she said. : "Your mother's conditions ? " "Yes."

- He was silent. He aid not ask her how she came, or why. She was there before him, and he felt that the great moment of his life had come. "What are we going to do ? " she asked. He continued silent. Her presence made him long more than ever to yield to the conditions which had been made, and yet she quickened his sense of duty, and of loyalty to what was right. "Has he—Ritzoom--told you of our meetings?" he asked presently.

MY DRAB NELLIE,

It will not surprise you, I ween, to hoar that both light and black coats will certainly be worn over coloured dresses this autumn, and one would look very well over a shepherd's plaid dress. Plain skirts will also be worn, and they look better than fancy ones in plaid materials, and it is best to choose a bodice with seams only on the shoulders and under the arms, as they do not look well in checked materials. One of the best materials for winter shirts is viyella. Stiff collars are not worn as much as they used to be, but they are still fashionable. Many people prefer a soft silk stock or tie. It is not usual to wear straw hats in winter, and a black velvet or glace silk hat would look better. Sometimes it is possible to buy shapes ready-made in silk, satin, or velvet. Tucked black glace silk would be nice as a trim. ruing, and very durable, and perhaps you have some ostrich tips. If you do not want a silk underskirt, moirette or a fine moreen would be best for winter wear. Black moirettes with narrow satin stripes in a bright colour are very effective.

Birds and their plumage appear largely on the new models, wings and quills being treated with further adornments, till the bird they had their origin with would certainly disown them. Alas, that not all the dictates of humanity can induce women to forego the curious pleasure of bearing about on their heads the evidences of ruthless cruelty which give the lie direct to their reputed qualities of mercy. The bird of paradise, placed in the centre of a broad-brimmed, low-crowned hat, with the feathers drooping on either side of the face, is another revival which bears us back in mind to the caricatures of the dress of 50 years ago. Hats and toques alike fit the I closely-at the back, and are wide in front, with low trim- mings. The hair underneath the hat is dressed closely and neatly at the back, with very little fulness and only a suspicion of waving. In front it is hidden by the low bent hat brim, or, if the latter turns back, as is the fashion with some hats, off the face, the hair is shown turned back off the forehead in loose waves. -

read

Amongst some pretty blde muslin bodices is one made without any collar, the top of the bodice being edged with a little ruching of soft blue silk, matching the belt. Another muslin had a trans- parent lace collar with a single line of narrow black velvet run through it to keep it up. All soft materials continue to be gathered at the waist, some all round, others at the back only. It is very easy to cut out and make one of the latter, and they are very becoming, much more so than the skirts that have but a single pleat at the bark for fulness. The front breadth is gored, but the others are left full. In mounting it into the band the front of the skirt is slightly eased into the band round as far as where the sides turn towards the back. Then come the gathers which have to be done with three runnings and very neatly finished off. In sewing them on the belt the sewer must take up each separate gather on the needle and give it a stitch to itself. Such a skirt as this can be cut out and run up in a few hours, and there it is, ready to put on. A black barege, for instance, can be begun after breakfast and finished in the afternoon. To be able to make it at home justifies one in buying an inexpensive dress length, for making which it seems out of all proportion to pay double what it cost.

Women are beginning to learn how really smart flannel may be, if deftly allied to velvet and lace. One of the blouses I have seen is made of striped orchid purple flannel, has a bolero front edged with much deeper purple velvet, and is worn with a pointed lace collar and vest. Another is composed of soft silk and lace, with bars of silk threaded through with very narrow satin ribbon. When silk is threaded in this way the incisions are button-holed to make them look neat, quaint and pretty. With both shirts for morning wear twilled serge skirts are worn. Your loving_

-JENNIE.

IREAKFAST AND SUPPER.

LOCOMOTIVE MEN'S GRIEVANCES.

Meeting at Warrington.

_ The Workmen's Club, Market Place, was filled on Sunday afternoon on the occasion of a joint meeting in connection with the local branches of the Anesciated Society of Locomotive Eneiceers and Firemen and the Amalgamated Society of Railway Servants. The business of the meeting was the consideration of the London and North-Western Locomotive Men's movement. Mr. Bald- win, of Liverpool, presidsd and there were also present on the platform: Mr Thorehill, of Swansea, secretary for the movement; Mr Baguley and Mr Bellamy, of Stockport. Mr J EPlinston, Mr Greaves, Mr Taylor and Mr Fryer, of Warrington. The CHAIRMAN pointed out in the course of a short speech, that on the Great Central line' on which he was a driver, they worked 11 hours per day. Despite the long hours they were in a better position than the Lon- don and North-Western Locomotive men inasmuch as ' they bad a guaranteed wage. (Hear, hear.) Mr. PLINSTON read the reply of the directors of the London and North-Weetern Railway Company in respect of the proposals submitted to them by the loco men. The reply stated that the memorial had been considered by the directors,and they declined to agree to the proposals which were put forward, and did not consider that there were any grounds for a meeting for discussion. Most if not all of the questions now raised were not very long ago considered and decided by the directors alter con- ference with some of the men. Mr. Plinston proceeded to propose the following resolution :—" That this meeting of London and North-Western locomotive men earnestly requests that the Board of Directors will grant an interview with our duly elected representatives, with a view of discussing with the directors our present conditions of employment and future employment." ee Mr GREAVES seconded the resolution. He said he was confident tbat the locomotive men werelustified in ask- ing for better conditions of service. The locomotive men were treated with contempt in a great many instances, and they were the only grace of railway men so treated. Mr. Tnomenite, who bad a cotdial reception, spoke at length on the proposals which were submitted to the directors of the London and North Western Railway Company. These included the following :-.—That engine drivers, firemen and cleaners be granted a week's wage independent of Sunday duty ; eight hours to constitute a day's work and all time worked in excess of eight hours to be paid for at the rate of time and a quarter, each day to stand by itself and all men to be paid a day's wage each time of signing on duty ; 150 miles or part thereof to constitute a day's work for expreis passenger and fish trains; 100 miles or part thereof to constitute a day's work for express goods and meat trains ; no man to be called on duty who has not bad 10 hours rest from the time of signing off duty ; Sunday duty to be paid for at the rate of double time. The following proposed scale of wages was also included in the men's proposals; drivers to receive first year 63 per day; second year, 6s ed per day; third year. 7s per day ; fourth year, 7s 6i per day ; fifth year, Ss per day. Firemen's wages, first year, 43 per dry ; second year, 4s fild per day ; third year, 4s 91 per day ; fourth year, 5i per day ; sixth year, 5s Oct per day. Cleaners, 3s per day after 17 years of age ; shed labourers, 25s per week. Proceeding to refer to the truck system,the spealteressid it bed increased the duties of locomotive men without any corresponding extra re- muneration and was also a source of numerous accidents. It was time the truck system was abolished even from a humanitarian point of view. Mr. Thornhill then read a copy of the letter he had sent to Mr, F. W. Webb with reference to their decision on the men's memorial. The letter stated that the men considered that their pro- gramme had not received at the hands of the Board that consideration which they as an important biedy of men felt they bad a right to expect. They'asked for an in- terview to be arranged between the men's representa- tives and the Board for the purpose of discussing with them the present conditions of aer vice with a view to an improvement. It was pointed out that it was two years last March since the representatives of the men met the representatives of the Board and the result of the interview was a concss- sion in wages to a small percentage of firemen. Since then the duties of the men in the locomotive department I had increased considerably in consequence of the i enormous increase of traffic. The actual necessaries of life bad increased in cost to such an extent that it was impossible for men to maintain themselves and families on what they now received for service rendered to the company. Mr :Thornhill added that the receipt of his letter had been acknowledged and he was informed that it would come before the directors at their October meeting. Ho thought if they could get an interview with the directors their grievances would receive more consideration. There was no desire on the part of the men to strike. They did not wish to upset the trade of the company any more than the directors did. (Hear, hear.) Mr. BAG/LEY, in supportinethe resolution, Raid he was glad to attend such a successful Hut meeting of engine-men. The disputes which had arisen between the two societies had been a source of weakness in the past and he was glad to know that their differences had now been healed. (Hear. hear.) Referring to the remark of Lord Stalbridge that the coal bill of the London and North-Western Railway Company had increased by £64,000 in the past six months, the speaker said suffi• cient proroiceece was not given to the tact that this was partly due to the engines having run half a million miles more than in the preceding six months. (Hear, bear.) Alter referring to scme of the grievancss of locomotive men he said the men were determined to have the conditions altered. They did not desire a strike but even a 7; n7m when trampled upon would turn. He suggested that the executive should draw up a list of questions to Le submitted to Parliamentary 1 candidate relating to the Board of Trade's action with railway companies. (Hear, hear.) The resolution was . unanimously carried. Mr. BELLAMY then proposed a resolution approving of the men's delceates and assuring thena of hearty sup- port in the future. With this end in view members _ were urged to sign the signature e beets and call upon non-unionists to join their respective societies. Mr. FRYER seconded and Mr. GREAVES supported. The resolution was unanimously adopted. A vote el thanks having bean tendered the deputation on the proposition of Mr. TAYLOR, the preceedings terminated.

THE TEMPTATION OF DUNCAN RUTLAND.

By JOSEPH HOCKING,

Author of "The Birthright," "Mistress Nancy Molesworth," "All Men are Liars." "The Story of Andrew Fairfax," ac.

:CHAPTER XXXL DUNCAN RUTLAND'B TEMPTATION.

—"Well, and you enjoyed your visit to the Eternal' City?" said Ritzoom, with a pleasant smile. ' "Enjoyed is scarcely the word," replied the young man, and he sat back in his armchair, while opposite him sat the Jesuit smoking a large cigar. They were in Rutland's study on the Sunday evening as had been arranged. The young minister was rather excited. This was scarcely to be wondered at, for his work through the day had been very trying. Great eager crowds had filled the spacious building, and the young man's whole strength had gone out to the services. Moreover, he guessed the object of the priest's visit, and felt sure he would have some message from Lady Neville.

"Isn't it? Well, perhaps not. One enjoys a cigar, but scarcely a visit to Rome, that is if one possesses the historical spirit; one is awed, bewildered, overwhelmed, but one does not enjoy' in the ordinary sense of the word." Rutland waited for him to proceed. "Of course, you will have guessed that I had some definite purpose in this visit?" said Ritzoom, after a short silence.

"Yes, I gues,sed that." "Of course you did. Well, there are two traits of my character which are strongly marked. One is an utter frankness, and the other is a disposition to get at a point quickly." Rutland nodded.

"Well, to be true to my character, I will be frank, and I will get to the point at once. lam sure this will be most pleasing to you as a straight- forward Englishman." The young man again nodded, but remained silent.

"I will, therefore, be brutally blunt. You have, I am given to understand, become the suitor for the hand of our mutual friend, Miss Alizon Neville, and that she has been disposed to listen kindly to you? " The young man flushed rather angrily. He did not relish the priest's interposition in the matter, but he spoke no word. "Of course," went on Ritzoom, "I have personally no right to interfere with your privacy; but as you may know, I am honoured with Lady Neville.'s confidence, and—well, it has always been a pleasure for me to meet you." "Thank you," replied Rutland, quietly. "I will be brutally frank," laughed Ritzoom, almost nervously, "and I will confess that I hoped y ur visit to Rome would have led you to embrace our faith; but my hopes have not been realised." "No, if you hoped that, they have not been realised ; they never will." "That is a pity. Lady Neville's objections might be overruled then."

Rutland looked up inquiringly. "Yes, I repeat it. Personally, mind you, I should rejoice in such an union. I am not influenced by English prejudices. I believe in the aristocracy of intellect, the aristocracy of worth, but, of course, we don't all see alike, do we? " "No, we don't all see alike." "And so I have come to see whether we cannot arrange this matter. We are men, and we are both broad-minded enough to see the value of com- promises. One of your sceptical philosophers seems to think that compromise is the basis of civilisation."

"Am I to understand then," said Rutland, roused to speak by Ritzoom's words, "that Lady

"Will certainly disinherit her if she persists in disobedience. And, well, even if you were to— well, carry out the plans which have suddenly formed, themselves in your mind, it would be terribly cruel to drag her from the Priory in order that she might share with you the pleasures of a ct ttage." "I presume you have something to suggest," remarked Rutland, presently. "Yes," replied Ritzoom, "I have, and I feel sure you will see the reasonableness of my proposals. You see I wish to do two or three things. I wish, first of all, to save Alizon from being driven from her home, and I wish to make both of you happy. This, you will understand, cannot be done unless we adopt a course :,grePable to Lady Neville." "Well, tell me what you wish to say? " "Of course, I need not remind you that you are a Protestant., and that Lady Neville is a Catholic. Now, while, in time, her ladyship may become reconciled to the thought of her daughter marrying a Protestant, she would never consent to her marry- ing a Protestant to ir "1 see."

"Of course, such an arrangement, would be absurd ; indeed, her ladyehip, with her antecedents and her devoted loyalty to the Church, would rather see her daughter dead than that such a thing should happen." "Well ?" Rutland's face was set and stern, his eyes shone with an angry light. "You see that. Well, the proposal lam authorised to make is this: You resign Tudor Church, and study for another profession, say the bar. With your previous education and training, you would be able to pass the necessary examina- tions very quickly. Then when you are entitled to wear silk, her ladyship will be willing to listen to your proposal for her daughter's hand." "And meanwhile ? " Rutland asked the question as though it meant much to him. "Meanwhile," answered Ritzoom," her ladyship will not seek to prejudice her against yOu." "But should I be allowed to see her ? " "That, cf course, would not be wise." "But if I remain a Protestant minister?" "Then, of course, the case is hopeless. Even you can see that it would not be possible for her to consent to her daughter receiving the attentions of a Protestant minister." "But if I persist, and—nnd if she_persists ? "

"In that -case I think you would, to use an English colloquialism, cut your own throat.' You would cause the woman you love to be disinherited, for her ladyship would no longer own her as her daughter. More than that, you would blight the lives of both mother and child. Her ladyship possesses the spirit of the old Roman matrons. She will do what she believes is right, but the evening of her life would he darkened, her home would be practically destroyed. You can, of course, see this. But not only would you curse her ladyship's life, you would curse her daughter's as well. How could she be happy while the memory of her dis- obedience haunted her ? " - . _

"Have you Lady Neville's authority for saying this?

"Yes, I have; that is, I have her authority for making this proposal which I submitted to you. Have I made everything plain? " "Yes, I think so." "You can, of course, see what a great concession it is on her ladyship's part. Indeed, I never thought she would have yielded so much." "There is nothing more to say, I suppose? " "No, nothing. The issues are very simple, and I hope 1 may be able to congratulate you on de- ciding wisely." "I hope so." "Well, what do yousay ? What answer shall I take to her ladyship? " "None at all—at present." "Oh, well, of course there is no hurry ; all the same, I thought you would have had no difficulty in deciding. You are not asked to change a single article of your faith, you are simply required to change one profession for another, and a far more desirable one. Why, think! The successful barrister may rise to the highest position in the land. Ho may be a great advocate, or even a judge. He may go into Parliament and rise to be Chancellor of the Exchequer, or even First Lord of the Treasury. And with Lady Neville's influence, a baronetcy, or even a peerage, is not impossible. On the other hand, what has the Nonconformist ministry to offer a man of your ability and ambi- tion? You can rise no higher than you have alrearly risen. You are the minister of the prin- cipal dissenting church in the town, and if you stay here for thirty years—well, you can be no more. I think I speak as your friend, and I do not see how a sane man could reject the proposal 1 have made, and to which her ladyship has con- sented. You see this, don't you? "Yes, I see it." "If you refuse—well, I see nothing but misery as the result. Whatever course events take, they must be calamitous; but I heed not go over that gronnd again." . . .

===== 0002601_19000928_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
"Yes, be has told me everything, told it as only he can tell." He nodded. "What are we going to do ? " she asked again presentli• , He caught her hand:— "0 Alison!" he cried," it would be worse than death to—to give you up." "Would it ? " and a bright light shone in the girl's eyes. "Yes," he said, and she knew that he meant what he said.

They were both silent for a few seconds, for they knew not what to say. "You must not give up your Church," she said presently. "Must I not ? " His voice was hoarse, for the girl's words sounded like a death-knell in his heart. "No, it would be wrong, it would be disloyal,you must not lower your standard by adopting a poorer profession." "No." He felt she was right, knew that her voice was as the voice of God.

"All those fine sayings about place, position, and power are as nothing," she said, "they cannot be entertained by those who believe as we do. We know the truth, and the truth has set us free."

He felt ashamed of himself. She was a convert of only a few weeks, and yet she taught him his duty. "Then you have come to bid me good-bye," he said presently. "What ? " There was a sob in her voice as she uttered the word.

"You have come to bid me good-bye," he repeated. "Oh, I know what you say is right. I feel it more than ever now. But, 0 Alizon, it will be like death to part with you." The girl looked at him with questioning, yearn- ing eyes. "Yes, like death," he repeated. "The thought that I must spend my life without you, month after month, year after year, and yet not to have you near me. 0 God, I cannot bear it!" "Must you bear it ? " and her voice trembled as she spoke. "The man said there as no other way, and I feel that he is right. It would be mean of me to ask you disobey your mother; it would be wrong of me to try and alienate you from home, and cause you to be disinherited. Oh, I feel the meanness, the unmanliness of doing such a thing. And even if I thought you would consent I would not ask you. I have fought it all out, I know the bitterness, the remorse that would follow in the train of such an action, and yet-0 Alizon, may God give us strength to bear it." "Is there no other way? " said the girl. "No, I know no other. You have made me feel that I cannot cease being a minister, and I cannot ask you to do the other." "Then I must ask you, Duncan ? " He turned towards her with a startled, wondering look in his eyes. "If you will not ask me, I must ask you," she repeated. "God did not lead us to love each other to mock us. He did not open our eyes that we should live in darkness. I have been thinking, too. I—l cannot believe that mother will remain for ever angry with me, she loves me too much for that. And even if she does, well, do you think I care so much for house and position and society and all that sort of thing—as to, oh, don't you understand ? "

The young man sat like one dazed. He knew not what to say, or what to think. "I should blight your happiness for ever if— if--" He stopped suddenly. "And if you don't ? " "If—if 1 don't!" he stammered. "Yes, if you don't, what would my life be then? "

He did not speak a word. "Can you refuse me, Duncan?" she said, looking into his eyes. "No, I cannot. God forgive me, but I cannot," he cried, as he saw the light of her great love shining through her tear-dimmed eyes. [Tun END. I

Combination in the Pork Trade.

A large and succes-ful meeting of master pork butchers of Liverpool and district was held at the Be Hotel, Liverpool, on Monday evening with the object-. of organising under the superintendence of Mr. Wolfenden, of Warrington, an association to look after all matters of interest affecting the trade. Mr. John Voch was voted to the chair. The follow- ing officers were appointed ;—President, Mr D Ben- bet: vice-presidents, Mr P Darken, Mr R Hodson, and Mr Brundrett ; hon. secretary. Mr James Hod- son; organizing secretary. Mr T L Wolfenden; treasurer. Mr Robb; and a committee consisting of , twelve members of the trade. Arrangements were made for the first annual dinner of the Association. A copy of suggested rules was submitted by Mr. Wolfenden and adopted. Short speeches were delivered by several of those present appealing for unity of action and combination in the trade. A vote of thanks was tendered to the Chairman and a similar compliment was paid to Mr Wolfenden who had taken a great amount of trouble in bringing the members together. A promising start was made and a large number of subscriptions were re- ceived.

THE FREE CHURCHES.

MISLEADING ANGLICAN STATEMENTS.

The Rev. Thomas Moore, M.A., has written a very misleading article in the Church Monthly "Church and Denominational Statistics. Mr. Moore repeats the inaccurate statement that Nonconformists have claimed that their numbers are fully half, if not the greater part, of the population. Nonconformists do nothing of the kind. They claim that with regard to membership they aro nearly as strong as the Established Church, and with regard to Church accommodation and to the number of Sunday-school scholars and teachers they are much stronger. This claim has been demon- strated in figures which have never been seriously challenged. We lament profoundly that the bulk of the population in England is outside all the Churches.

===== 0002601_19000928_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
THE NONCONFORMIST POSITION.

We confess we are surprised that Mr. Moore should say that Nonconformists refer to the Registrar-General's list of places for Noncon- lormist worship as a proof of their assertions. On the other hand, quite the contrary. Our figures are gathered from perfectly reliable statistics supplied by the Nonconformist Churches themselves. Nonconformists object to a religious census for perfectly intelligible and conclusive reasons. They say that it is no real test of the strength of the Churches in the community. Hundreds and thousands of people who do not go anywhere would set them- selves down as "Church." In the next place, they strongly object to any inquisition'lby the State into individual religious opinion. It is all very well for Church statisticians to sneer at figures with regard to Church attendance and Church membership; but can they devise any better means of ascertaining the strength of a religious denomination ?)

===== 0002601_19000928_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SIMULTANEOUS MISSION, 1901.

The preparations for the Simultaneous Mission,'organised by the National Free Church Council, are now in full swing. It is interest- ing to observe that it has been suggested that ladies should take part in the active work of the mission. There are many women who are admirably fitted for public work, such for instance as Miss M. Jennie Street, who at the recent Christian Endeavour Convention made a very bright and interesting speech. In addressing children's meetings, or perhaps by visiting the .people in their own districts, women may do a great deal to secure the .success of this great endeavour. It is felt by the leaders of the movement that every person who is lihely to be of service must be pressed into this great and critical work. Everybody can do someOling. All the available forces should be concentrated into a determined attack on the strongholds of Satan.

DR. ?..',ACLAIIEN AND THE COUNTILY MINISTERS.

Dr. ?.41aelaren, of :',lanchester, opened a bazaar at Grantown, in the Nor:h of Scotland. The little lllpt,ist church there was founded nearly a hundred years ago, the result of the preach- ing of the brothers Haldane. Dr. Maclaren said that there were few men who deserved more nobly of their country than the ministers and doctors of these rural districts. With little exciernent, with little change, in danger of stagnation from being cut off from all the sources of mental and spiritKal qui3kening, they yet nobly and persistently year by year carried on their unrecognised toil.

THE OPENING OF THE METROPOLITAN TABER- NACLE.

The services at the re-opening of the Metro- politan Tabernacle were very successful. The first gathering, which was a large prayer meet- ing, was crowded. The Rev. Archibald J. Brown, one of the late Mr. Spurgeons students, led the devotions of the assembly. The Rev. John Thomas. who has just recovered from a serious illness, preached in the afternoon to a large congregation. In the afternoon the crowd, gathering early, became so great that in order to avoid trouble the doors were opened at half - past five and the building was full at six. Those who had no tickets assembled in large numbers outside, and passed the time in singing hymns. The overflow meeting was held in the hall below the chapel, accommodating 2000 persons. Sir George Williams made a brief speech at this gathering. Mr. Sankey sang solos, at the three services. The interior of the new chapel is brighter than the old one was. and the electric light has been installed. The full cost has amounted to about f. 44,864, and over ;C45,000 has been received. Such a condition of things must be a great joy and aratitication to the devoted pastor of the church, the Rev. Thos. Spurgeon.

SHOULD CHINESE CHRISTIAN MARTYRDOMS BE AVENGED

The Free Church Chronicle for October con- tains a discussion and some interesting letters on this subject. The writers include Mrs. Bishop, the famous African traveller; the Rev. Frederic Galpin, President of the Metho- dist Free Churches, and for many years a missionary in China; the Rev. W. E. Soothill, who has written some brilliant articles in the Daily News on the Chinese missionary pro- blems ; the Rev. R. Warlaw Thompson, Foreign Secretary of the London Missionary Society; and the Rev. Dr. Wonyon. There are a variety of opinions expressed on the matter. Some of the writers bold absolutely that missionaries should go entirely on their own risks and that they should entirely abandon any legal claim to protection they may have on the Governments of their countries from which they come. Others hold this view with some important modifications. The whole corre- spondence, we think, is well worth a most careful study. It seems to be beyond doubt that no missionaries—or very, very few missionaries of the Societies they represent— have ever applied to her IVNesty's Government for the protection of the gunboat.

===== 0002601_19000928_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
THE REV. WILLIAM CUFF.

We are glad to know that the President of the Baptist Union has returned to his pulpit at the Shoreditch Tabernacle after being away for twelve months. He was heartily con- gratulated. The air of Norway has done wonders for him. From the beginning to the end of his first service his voice sounded clear. He was evidently in high spirits at being back again.

===== 0002601_19000928_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LATE ALDERMAN S. D. MEANDER.

Congregationalists SN'ill deeply regret the death of the Mayor of Wolverhampton. Mr. Mander was a great friend of the late Dr. Berry, of whose church, Queen-street, he was a deacon. He fulfilled the office of Mayor with great - -tisfaction to his fellow citizens, and two months ago he had the honour of enter- taining- the Duke and Duchess of York on their visit to the borough. This is the first occasion on which a Mayor of Wolverhampton has died during his year of office. Dr. Horton conducted in a most impressive manner the funei al service in the Queen-street Church._

Everyone will rejoice to hear that Mr. J. G. Clarke, Editor of the Christian World, has quite recovered his health during his recent holiday in Scotland.

THE 'NONCONFORMIST TRIUMPH.

The school started by Northampton Noncon- formists in St. James's End, in protest against the Education Department's refusal to sanction a School Board, is crowded to the fullest limit by scholars who were locked out of the Church school for wearing Nonconformist medals. St. James's End will shortly be incor- porated in the borough, and be under the School Board, which it is anticipated will im- mediately take steps to erect the desired Board School.

===== 0002601_19000928_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
JUBILEE OF BOLD-STREET WESLEYAN CHURCH,

.Ei:

===== 0002601_19000928_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
Visit of the Mayor and Corporation.

It was only fitting that the jubilee of Bold-street 1 Wesleyan Church should be celebrated in a manner worthy of the occasion, and the jubilee thanksgiving services on Sunday were such as to mark the event as one of the most notable in the history of local Methodism. The Mayor and Corporation by their at- tendance at the morning service gave an officill recogni- tion to the event and to some extent showed their appre- ciation of the services of many members of the Wes- leyan denomination in the public affairs of the town and neighbourhood. The procession left the Town Hall at a quarter past ten and was headed by a posse of police, followed by the firemen. Then came the pic- turesque figures of the sword and mace bearers preceding the Mayer, who had the Member for the Borough on his right and the Rev John Yonge (his chaplain) on his left. The members of the Corporation in attendance were Aldermen Francomb, Heaton and Hutchinson; Council- lors Peacock, Plumpton, A J Jolley, Alfred Jolley, Pierpoint. Forshaw, Burton, John Roberts, Ellison, Rogers, Davies, Smethurst, Wilkinson, and Baird ; and the officials: Messrs Stanley Wilson (deputy town clerk), John Fairhurst (borough treasurer), W H Woodcock (assistant treasurer and collector), T Longdin (borough survevor),G F F White (deputy clerk to the magistrates), J H Gornall (medical officer), Elijah Place (markets inspector), W S Haddock (gas engineer), C Madeley (secretary, librarian curator. &c.) A:number of promi- nent townsmen brought up the rear: On arriving at the church, the Mayor and Corporation immediately took up the seats in the centre of the church, reserved for them. During the while, the organist (Mr. R. Hickman) played the National Anthem, and the vast congregation stood up. The preacher for the occasion was the Rev. F. W Macdonald, ex-president of the Wesleyan Conference. He selected for his text the words I exhort, therefore, that, first of all, supplications prayers, intercessions, and giving of thanks.be made for all men ,for kings,and for all that are in authoriteothat we may lead a quiet and peaceable life in all godliness and honesty." 1. Tim. ii., 1,2. He said the text urged them to lead a quiet and peaceable life, They were further taught that the possibilities of such a life were largely determined by the action and influence of particular individuals on whom depended the condi- tions of life, social and moral, for vast numbers of other people—kings, rulers, leaders, those of influence, those of authority. They were also taught that all human affairs were within the proper range of intercessory prayer, that prayer should be offered to God not only for the personal interests of the particular suppliant, not only for interests that were sp, cifically spiritual and had immediate reference to salvation, but that prayer should be offered with wide sympathv and a broad desire for the welfare of man as man. These points the preacher proceeded to elaborate, and remarked that to many the idea of a quiet and peaceable life sounded altogether too tame, was pitched too low, was lacking in boldness. colour, breadth. To young people for example, with all the instincts and characteristics of youth, words like those he had quoted might produce a sense of disappointment, and be considered an ideal for the chimney corner for the timid and enfeebled, but were lacking in adventurous spirit and colour. Such an idea was rather repellent than attractive to those who were living in vicious ways and were dissipated in spirit. But it was not necessarily a tame and poor-spirited programme, not necessarily a life of torpor, of intellectual stagnation, of social dulness and feebleness. The whole spirit and teaching of Christianity could not be invoked for the sanction of such ideas, but their wise and large-hearted teacher of Christianity did desire that the Info of men generally should be set free from those main evils which weighed, oppressed and distressed, crippled and wounded the best elements of life. What a vast advance there would be in happiness for the innumerable peoples of the earth if life could be described in terms akin to that ! There were great cemmunitiee afflicted by famine and pestil- tnoe. There were whole races with whom the struggle for existence was constant, painful, who were haunted by fears and plunged below the level of comfort and happiness. There were great portions of the earth where blood was peured out as water. Think of the fair districts that had been battlefields within the last year, of the vast provinces where now there was confu- sion, the overturning of all the sweet proprieties and amenities of human life. Think of the whole races of men, whose type of social life had never yet tisen above a constant earthly warfare, enslav- , merit and massacre of the population, where anything like security and comfort was unknown, where there was no steady calm cultiva- tion of the domestic and social elements of human life. where fear, distress, battle, murder and sudden death were the continual characteristics. A quiet, peaceable life would be even now a vast promotion for a great number of people under the sun ; a not unworthy ideal, a thing that wise and good men might pray for when they spread out a map before them and reflected upon the condition of the population of the earth. There was nothing to be drawn from the Scriptures in favour of a dull and lethargic human existence, and they must not think of the quiet and peaceful life invoked in the text as a dull, unfruitful, and stagnant existence, but it was true that human life from which religion was omitted was nothing more than a frag- mentary or mutilated existence. bet him press this home. Suppose a community enjoyed peace, that its agriculture, commerce and manufactures possessed an ideal prosperity, its arms were victorious by land, and sea, and the conditions of its finances made it the very banking house of the world ; but if there was no moral and spiritual life touched with the fear of God,quickened and animated by the life of God, no holy aspirations in the minds of men nothing drawing that community to the pursuit of what was highest and best, would anyone contend that that spectacle of human development had realized the highest possibilities or could satisfy the human conscience? The ideal for the individual, the family, the town, the nation was a life of peace, of fruitful industry, intelligent cultivation of every broad and ennobling influence, a true religion which hallowed human nature,which lifted man above the passions that were ever seeking to deteriorate and degrade, and which gave him a source of moral strength- Those who had to keep the public order, to see to the proprieties and decencies of social existence, who look out upon life with the responsibilities of magistrates and governors, would pause before sanctioning a [social ideal from which the motives and restraints of religion amongst the people were withdrawn, and he had no hesitation in saying that there were communities in this modern world—it might seem ungracious to 'name them, and he would, therefore, let the reference be general—whose sceptical ruler would give anything to have amongst the masses those who believed in a living God, and those who were found in tens of thousands of homes in this little island of ours. Most of them could name a dozen men upon whom the great issue of world-wide peace or war depended—two or three emperors, a president or two, and hall--a-dozen statesmen, and there they had human beings upon whose sense of duty, upon whose moral enlightenment, upon whose convictions and character it largely depended whether there should be peace and quietness or war and confusien, whether the nation's life should be guided along paths that were honest and useful and full of progress, or dragged into the perilous highways of strife and evil. But he Would not limit his illustration even to kings and states- men of the earth, and the rev, gentleman in a masterly manner, proceeded to show the enormous influence for good or evil in the power of the individual and of small bodies of men, even boys on the playground being leaders in one direction or the other. He instanced four persons who had exercised an enormous influence during the present century upon the conduct of millions of people—the Prince Regent (afterwards King). Queen Victoria, Byron, and Tennyson. The noble life of the Queen, and the poems of Tennyson—not a line from which could be quoted that gave the sanction to vice, or that flattered the pride and conceit of selfish men and women—were contrasted with the pernicious influence set up by the Regent and Byron. They were of necessity in different camps, but when they prayed the boundary was met, the divided chasms filled up, party divisions vanished, natural antipathies and prejudices passed away, and there•was spread before the mind's eye a vast procession of mankind from east, west, north and south, speaking in different tongues,and yet all brothers in respect of sin, all brothers in respect of the mercy

THE X AMIN ER, URIDAY, SEPTEMBER 28, 1900.

and .00dwill of God and of the redemption made by our Lord Jesus Christ. Therefore, let theta pray, int for themselves and their households only, but for the dear land in which they lived, and then let their prayer move out in wider circles over larger areas t )wards the struggling suffering populations of the earth,that wisdom and guidance be given to all. A special mnsical programme had been arranged. The service commenced with the singing of the Sanctus (Spohr) in which Mr. Lvi Richardson sang a sok) part. This was followed by the singing of the hymn " All people that on earth do dwell." The "Te Deum " (Jackson) and the anthem "0 God my soul thirstetti " were also rendered. ===== 0002601_19000928_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
Young People's Service.

In the afternoon a great gathering of the scholars of the Bold-street, Silver-street, Sankey Bridges, and Elies- - Sunday Schools was held in the church. It wee announced that the chair would be taken by Mr. R Garnett, but, owing to the recent accident which befel him he was unable to be present. Mr. Lamplugh con- sented to take his place. kfter the singing ot a hemn. The Rev. H. G. ROBERTS, in introduciog the Clean- man. said he had been very closely associated with Methodism in that district for more than fifty years,and he thought they were particularly fortunate in having him present that afternoon. The Glummer; gave a short history of the rise and progress of the Wesleyan Church in Warrington, and in- formed his hearers that he came to Warrington in the year 1844 and worshipped at the old Bank-street Chapel, about six years betore the erection of the large building in Bold-street. At that time Warrington and Runcorn were one circnit. The resident ministers were the Rev. Joseph Burgess and the Rev. James Scholes, and every third Sunday the Runcorn minister preached in Warrington. He had a vivid recollection of the appointment, towards the close of the first half of the century, of the Rev Samuel Broadbent and the Rev George Dickenson to that circuit and it was through their excellent ministry, especially of the latter,that the old Bank-street church became too small to accommodate the worshippers. The speaker mentioned the names of some of the revered men and women who worshipped in the old Bank street Chapel, and who contributed to the foundation of the building in Bold-street. He said that Mr Guest, Mr Saaderson, Mr Thomas Dutton,and Dr Smith would ever be remem- bered for their noble devotion and unfaltering zeal in the erection of that church. For the opening of the church the services of three of the most eminent men in Methodism were secured. They were Dr Robert Newton, Dr Beaumont, and the Rev G C Macdonald, the father of the gifted ex-president of the Wesleyan Conference, whom they heard preach that morning. Mr. E. H. PEARSE Maw,'" Thou art passing hence "in a creditable manner, and a trombone solo was also con- eributed by Mr Smith. The Rev. H. G. ROBERTS expressed the pleasure it afforded him to be with them at a service which, he thought, marked a very important epoch in the history of local Methodism. It seemed to him that they were celebrating their jubilee services in a manner worthy of the Jews before the captivity. He was reminded by the strains of the trombone that the Jewish jubilee services were always proclaimed by the sound of the trumpet, and it might be new to the children to know that the first part of the word jubilee came from the word " Yobel "which meant the sound of the trumpet. By placing that item on the pro.; ramme Mr Hickman bad been wisely advised, as the trumpet or the jubilee had been sounded that day in a very appropriate manner. It seemed to bim that in that great gathering of young and old people, be saw, not simply in imagination, a jubilee. but a solemn convocation worthy to be remembered in history. He was pleased with the reminiscences the Chairman had given them. It had been his intention to dwell tipon their past history himself, but he thought he would reserve what he had to say* They could not pos- sibly separate themselves from the past. They had with them that afternoon the inheritors of the past and the hope of the future, and what the town of Warrington would be in the year 1950 depended very largely upon the children of the present generation. In the words of one of the poets they were told:the world made its own Progress on the feet of little children. Oa Saturday Mr Summersgill was laid to rest and next I:ileac:ley would witness the funeral of Alderman Blackburst, and on every hand they were reminded of the brevity of life. The hope of the future was in the nursery and the Sunday school, and not in the sepnlchre, however beautiful it might be. The speaker then proceeded to addre!s the scholars more particularly, and, in conclusion, remarked that it was a right and proper thing that they should meet together in that way to acknowledge God in songs and words of blessing, and in charity of heart and mind- He trusted that on that great day the members of this Church would endeavour to discharge the respon- sibilities with which they have been eetrusted, and ' uphold the traditions of the Church—a Church that had been involved in all the progress of the past 50 years, and shared in the advance, expansion, and moral spiritual progress of the town. Toe past was ever with them. It was a paradox to say that the pre- sent was the sum tetsl of their past, and the past was the sum total of that which had been their present. Tbe past was a sort of perpetual present. It shaped the present and moulded the future. No history of that Church could be written by God. If they took God's name out of the history of their Church, there re- mained nothing to tell. " God hath done great things for us, whereof we rejoice." The service was concluded by the singing of the National Anthem. Mr. Hickman presided at the organ. In the evening every seat was occupied, and many people were unable to obtain admission. Appropriate hymns were again sung, and before the sermon Mr Levi Richardson rendered a solo in an effective manner, Mr Frank Hickman accompanying on the organ. The Rev F W Macdonald preached from the words " Honour all men," and was again listened to with the keenest inter- est. The special feature of the musical p rt of the ser- vice was the rendering of " The Hallelujah Chorus," which was finely interpreted by the augmented choir. Before the close of the service the rev, gentleman announced that the collections for the day amounted to Ithe handsome sum of £73.

LOCAL FUNERALS.

The Late Alderman Blackhurst.

The remains of the late Alderman Blackhurst were interred in the Weaverliam Churchyard on Tuesday afternoon. The funeral was attended by the Mayor and Corporation and magistrates who assembled at the Town Hall at half-past ten, where carriages were in waiting. The cortege was beaded by a number of policemen under the direction of Chief Inspector Phillips. The Mayor was accompanied by the Town Clerk, and •,ther members of the Corporation present were Aldermen Fairclough (deputy-mayor), Sutton and Heaton ; Councillors Davies, Pluinpton, A Jolley, Baird, Forihaw, Edison, Pierpoint, and Smethurst. Amongst others in the carriages were the Borough Treasurer (Mr John Fairhurst), the Borough Surveyor (Mr. T Longdin), Mr Deas (water engineer), Mr, Haddock (gas engineer), Dr For, Mr J W Hayward, Mr H Thorn- ton, and Mr G D Southerden, of Sankey-street. The route taken was along linutsford-road to the late residence of the deceased, No. 1, Granpenhall-road, where the procession was joined by the mourners from the house, in which a short service, conducted by the Rev John Gunnell, bad just been held Toe cortege then proceeded on its way over the Cantilever Bridge, along the Grappenhall-road to the junction of the four roads at Stockton Heath. The mourners in their respective carriages continued ea their way to the church, the members of the Corporation, officials and magistrates returning to Warrington. The chief mourners were Miss Blackhunt, Miss Fiorrie Blackhurst (daughters), Mr and Mrn Parrett Ilfracombe (sonic-law and daughter) Mr limy Richardson (one of the deceased's oldest ser- vante) ; and Mr William Edison, of Bingswood, Prod- sham, the latter meeting the procession at Woaverham. The service was conducted by the Vicar of the parish. A number of beautiful wreaths were placed on tho coffin, which was of fumed oak with brass rnountings,and bore the inscription :—" George Blackhurst, born January 2,6 h, 1823, died September 10th, 1900." The funeral arrangements were el atistactorlly carried out by Messrs R Garnett and Sons.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Rugby Football. [By " Ovalist."]

Warrington were fairly beaten on Saturday, and as they had nrit expected such a fate at Stockport, the re- sult came as a disagreeable surpriae- It appoara that they have a bonus system at Stockport whereoy there is a difference of about laa. to the players between victory- and defeat, and so far the SCiIOLLIE3 seems ta have worked well, for Stockport are still undefeated and going very, strongly. There was no mistake about toeir determina- tion on Satuaday, and they fairly earned the extra half- sovereign.

Warrington were unfortunate in having to take the field minus Taylor and Fish, the abs"nce of the latter making a lot of difference, as his substitute, Duckwo:th, never made much progresa, although he worked hard and was ever on the alert. Fish would have had one or two profitable chances on Saturdayond then the reeu.t of the game might have been different. His presence mtkes a wonderful difference to the strength of the team, and even "Smack's" re-appearance on Saturday did not compensate for the absence of the" finny creature."

In addition to being defeated Warrington got a very severe " gruelling," and met the test set of forwards they have seia so far. I thought Warrington were pretty strong in this department but St. lieleas dis- pelled a few illuiions, while Stockport simply altered my opinion altogether. Though they got possession oftenest by smart heeling out, the Warrington front rank was simply out-classed in footwork and following-up. The backs had an experience fvf the latter similar to that experienced by the Salford "stars "at Wilder- spool. They were grassed with a bang oftener than not when they got the ban. Stockport know exictly how high to kick a ball for it to drop at tile feet of the speedy forwards, and they never failed to give Warrington trouble by these somewhat ele.mentary but effective methods of play.

The game was gimped bakire the biggest crowd I hava seen at Edgely Park, where they are poor sportsmen, if the team is losing. They were terribly in earnest too, and the way thcy shouted when four" clarets" pulver- ised one weak Warringtonian was distinctly blood- thirsty. On the stand, too, they had 93M0 ideas of the game which ought to los phonograpbed for the benefit of referees.

There was a peculiarly apathetic air about Warring— ton in the earlier stages of the game, and for vite 20 minutes they didn't show form worth a continental. Then an ill•judged punt by Duckworth gave Worsley and Robinson a clear run in, the wing man scoring cleverly at the corner. This did the visitors good and they roused up, only to see Robinson run behind the posts from an opening made by Simister and a few other torwards who happened to be "on side" at the right moment. Bevan landed a goal in additionsso that Warrington's cup of misfortune was about full. It was rather a lucky try and certainly won the game.

Robinson deserves credit for it, however, for he took the opportunity very smartly. It is a strange thing that Robinson always scores against Warrington, sometimes three tries, but always one or two. I never remember him failing yet when he has been playing,,and it was therefore with unhappy forebodings that I saw him entering the ground with the other members of the home team. Somehow or other I had hoped his frequent injuries would have kept him out of the game to counter- balance the absence of Fish. But no, he was there and did the usual damage to Warrni.ztos record. He is a fine wing, and seemed to play as well as ever on Satur- day.

Reverting to the game again, Warrington 903 n re- covered from the misfortunes which betel them, and smarter work by the backi raised hopes in the breasts of their supporters. Bate was as skilful and tactful as ever, and it was from one of his well-timed openings that IQherwold and Duckworth ran half the length of the field, the latter scoring at the corner after an excel- lent piece of combined play.

There was no score in the second half, in which Warrington were much more energetic and ought to have scored with a little extra smartness and ordinary luck. Stockport came just as near to increas- ing their margin, and the honours of this half were about even. Hallam, Lawless, and Bate were the best backs on the losing side, the full back playing a grand game against tremendous odds, for the home forwards were on him in a flash, Lawless was much the better of the two centres. Isherwood being quite off-colour. His swollen face may be some excuse, for he did not leek" fit" by any means. I hope he will tumble into form soon, for his smart play is necessary to make the threequarter line successful. Burgess also shaped badly but solely owing to the way in which he got tackled and passed clumsily when free. He certainly has a lot to learn in feeding the three-quarters. " Smack" was an good form and his jndicious play had a lot to do with the decent show Warrington managed to make. Bevan at half, Worsley and Robinson, at three-quarters, were the pick of the home/rem all three being both smart and speedy. For the forwards there can be nothing but praise. They were both stronger and faster than their opponents and will take a lot of beating anywhere.

I don't want to take the opportunity of Warrington's first defeat to find fault, but the fact remains that Warrington cannot go through the season with eight forwards, and one or two of those not quite class enough. We never ware so badly off for scram- magers. The "A" team does not contain one who may be considered a first-class reserve, while the first team is too light in this department. The committee will have to bestir them- selves and increase the number of forwards, or more than one defeat will be met with shortly. One or two big-boned north-cOuntrymen are the sort required, and they can be found.

St. Helens last on their own ground to Runcarn who played a far superior game all round. There were other surprises for home teams too. Salford and Leigh actually getting severely defeated by Rochdale Hornets and Millom respec- tively, while Oldham only just got the better of Barrow. The latter had tho best of the game but lacked scoring power. They will have to be reckoned with by several clubs yet. Broughton Rangers obtained their first victory at the expense of Wigan, Widnes succumb- ing to Swinton, who are going the strongest of any of the Lancashiro clubs.

A LIITLE HEROINE.

The anxieties that agitate parents when their children are out of sight are sufficiently nerve-shaking, bu!.; the dangers that the little ones may fall into even under be eyes of the most devoted mothers, must be watched for as earnestly. Often the warning is insignificant, and the mischief done before it is perceived. An incident illustrating the truth of the e remarks came to the netic3 of thee" Southend Observer," and a reporter was sent to verify the details. Edith Wo^d is a little girl of twelve years, whose par Ants live at Walden's Farm, Great Stambridge, near Southend on Sea. "Mrs Wood was at home when I paid my Visit." writes the pressman. "and she showed herself (pito pleased to talk about her little girl. I suggested to the mother that she should tell me the facts in her own words."

..Well," she began, " last year the child became very strange in her niove'ments, and before long I saw it meant something more serious than childish antics. I sent for a doctor, and he said at once she had St. Vitus' dance. He came to see her regularly, and prescribed for her, but she did not seem to mend at all. She suffered a great deal, in fact. She used to be a bright child full of fun, and always ready to play with her mates, but when attacked by thi3 complaint she would come home and sit na Aping by herself. She was affected all down the left sidetand her arm was always twitching, esnecially her writit and hand. Sometimes in the middle of the night she would waken us up with her screams. This went on for three or four mouths, till one day a neighbour, whose daughter had been seized just in the same way, advised me to try a cure which had made her own child well—Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people. I talked the matter over with my husband, and we agreed to try a box of the pills. I must say that the first box we had didn't seem to make any difference, but we bought another, and then Edith grew better rapidly. Altogether we had three boxes, and by the time the child had finished the last one she, had lost the complaint completely. lain sure that Dr. Williams' pink pills have cured my child effectually, because it's months now sinco Edith ceased taking them, and she hasn't had the slightest return of her ailment. She is now as bright as any child could be."

At the moment Edith raced up, eutrunning her brothers with whom she had been romping in a field hard by. She looked the, picture of health. It is a fatal mistake to assume that children have no nerves. In young or old, adults or children, any disease or disorder of the brain or spine is manifested through the nerves, causing such disorders as epilepcy, tits, paralysis, neuralgia, early decay of the faculties and functions in middle life. and St. Vitus' dance in child- hood or old age. Dr. Williams' pink pills have cured all these disorders,alsO the effects of imperfect nutrition and poor blood, such as rickets in children, rheumatism and sciatica in adults. But the pills are genuine only with full name. Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people, and are sold by Dr. Williams' medicine company, Holborn Viaduct. London, at two and 'ninepeuce a box, or Rix boxes for thitteen and nine. Imitation pink pills are a snare and a delusion, and are offered to the public only for the sake of extra prat to the tradesman.

Proportion per cent. of in-door and out-door pauperism : 1872, in. 15 1, out, 84'8; 1898, in. 19.3, out, 806; 1899, in, 19.2, out, 807; 1900. in, 20'3, out, 79'6. Rate per cent of p,uperisai on the population : 1872, 3-6; 1898, 2.1 ; 1899, 2.1 ;-1900, 2. Mr A R Norman, chairman of the Home Com- mittee intimated that the House Committee that morning had been occupied in the ,consideration of an order of the Local Government Board. He had heard a great deal of the Government red tapeism, but it seemed to him they would have to go far to b-,at the present case. The order was in reference to the "Creed Register." Under the present system the register had the names arranged alphabetically with columns for the recording the outgoing and incoming of the paupers, whilst the arrangement proposed by the Local Govern- ment Board entailed the writing of the inmate's name upon each occasion he re-entered the house. Mr Norman thought it should be pointed out to the Local Govern- ment Board that the Guardians considered they ware being put to the expense of getting a now book, when in their opinion they considered the existing system was good enough. In a further remark Mr Norman also referred to the fact that the Order of the lacal Govern- ment Board was dated 1868.

Mr Stokes : It is out of date. (Laughter.) The chairman said he had been into the matter and was like the litt'e boy " He would sooner die than adopt the Local Government Board's Order." (In- creased laughter.) After some further discussion the matter was deferred until after the next visit of Mr. Dansie (the poor law Union Inspector), with the view to obtaining his advice.
