PECULIAR DOCK ACCIDENT AT WESTON. A Man Killed. At the Runcorn Police Court, on Thursday, Mr. R. Dobson. coroner, held an inquest on the body of Thomas Dawson, a dock labourer, who met his death the previous day whilst following his employment.—Mr. L. Rosenberg represented the Weaver Navigation Trustees ; Mr. F. A. Lake solicitor, the Ocean and Accident Insurance , Co.:Limited ; and Mr. W. Renny, Connah's Quay the shipowners. Mary Gallimore, 91, Percivals-lane, Runcorn, wife of George Gallimore, salt labourer, identified the body as that of her step-brother Thomas Dawson, who lodged with her during the last fortnight. Johe Alfred Done, 54, Shaw-street, Runcorn, dock labourer, swore that on Thursday, he was helping DAW- son with others to unload a schooner, named" Annie Jones," which was in the" Delemere " Dock. at Weston Point. Toe cargo was blue clay. At the time the tide , was out and the dock was " dropped " technical I term for the lowering of the water °Mho lock to thel level of the Ship Canal). Witness described how the unloading was commenced at about :seven o'clock. The clay was raised in baskets out of the hold by means of the ship's winch and tackle attached to the ship's masts. The clay was then empted into barrows, upon a stage of eight planks con,tructed upon trestles and subsequently wheeled off to be weighed. The stage was constructed on trestles along the quay, and the end near the vessel was resting upon a spar suspended:from the vessel's rigging. There was tackle for the purpose of regulating the height of the spar according to the movement of the vessel. When the water in the dock was lewered, how- ever, the stage end fell, and he and Dawson were pre— cipitated upon the vessel's deck. Witness only acarred himself, but did not know what became of Dawson. He only saw him fall. He had been instructed by Mr. Cowley, the berthing-master, to be " careful " and when the dock was being raised to lower the stage and when the water was going out they were to raise it. They did raise the stage when the water was coming in.—By Mr. Rosenberg. The vessel was moored at both ends, and when the dock was lowered the moor- ings were slackened. When the vessel was being raised again there was a lot of slack rope. He did not see any of the crew go to take in tbe " slack," and consequently the vessel " came away" from the quay side, causing , the planks to become detached from the spar and fall. lie had taken part in the erection of the stage,and knew all the planks were safe and sound ones. It was his opinion, that had the mooring of the vessel been pro- perly attended to the accident would not have occurred. —By Mr. Lake: The clay was raised from the hold by the ship's crew, and not by any tackle belonging to the docks.—By a:Juror The planks protruded over the suspended spar about a foot. When he got all right after falling, he found the vessel was about three feet from the quay. Deceased and birnself were responsible for the raising and lowering of the spar, but he thought it was the deck hands who were responsible for the moorine of the vessel. George Cowley, berth master, at the Weston Point dock, testified to seeing the "Annie Jones "in the dock on Thursday. Deceased and the last witness spoke to him about berthing the vessel the previous afternoor. Subsequently it was done, and the vessel was moored with two ropes fore and aft. He gave instructions to deceased and Done after the manner described by the last witness.—By Mr. W. Rennes? : He gave instructions to the mate of the vessel to look after his "bowsprit " when the dock would be raissd or lowered. That was all the instructions be gave to the vessel hands. Joseph Wakefield, 45, Bentick-street, Runcorn, weigher in the employ of the Weaver Navigation Trustees, said he saw the stage fall. Done and deceased were upon it ; but he only saw the deceased fall. Deceased fell upon the ship's deck. He saw the " spar " go away from the stage, and the stage fall upon the vessel's deck and the quay side. He went to the side of the vessel, but could not get on as it was away from the quay side. Dr. Andrew McLennan, of Runcorn, said he was called to see the deceassd at Westen Point,about half-past nine on Thursday. He found he was recently dead. On examining the body he found a contwed bleeding wound at the back of tho head ; there was also blood issuing from the nostrils, and in his opinion the cause of death was " fracture of the base of the skull." After a brief retirement, the jury returned a verdict of "accidental death," and added a rider that they were of opinion the operations of discharging when of that nature should be under the direction of a responsible person. Mr. Rosenberg said he would see that the jury's recommendation was forwarded to the proper quarter. - Mr. Dobson took the opportunity of thanking Mr.' Rosenberg for the sketch of the scene of the disaster he had provided for the use of the Court ; without it he was afraid they would have had some difficulty in under- standing the situation.

tettr:; to tbr ebitor.

Mr. Pierpoint's Election Address.

Sir,—This document is an exemplary specimen of brevity and reticence, and is embellished with three flags and a crown to stimulate the loyalty of his fol- lowers. The ex-member has had some eight years ex- perience of the light and leading of Lord Salisbury mud his henchman, Mr. Joseph Chamberlain and he saoriA, therefore, be in a position to lighten our darkness as to the benefits conferred upon the nation by those men. But with the peculiar modesty which befits him so well he contents himself by saying, "I might with confidence refer to the many measures which have been passed by Her Majesty's Government during the Parliament now ending, for the welfare of the country at large, and the working classes in particular." The latter part of this sonorous sentence provokes a smile. Is there any elec- tor so benighted that he does not know that the Salis- bury Administration have not even touched the fringe of the great wrongs under which the masses of the country are Buffering. What have they done for the landless millions who have been divorced from the soil , by unjust laws framed for the benefit of I the classes ? What have they done to ameliorate the ! closing days of the aged poor whose patient toil built op I the wealth upon which Lord Salisbury and his friends ! live in idleness and luxury ? What have they done to ! remove the huge injustice of a Parliamese-made Church which outrages the conscience and the rights of Christian men who utterly repudiate its spurious claims? And what have they done to put down, or even limit, the ravages of the liquor traffic, which are every day becoming moro aggressive, and threaten, sooner or later, ! !to engulf the' country in perdition ? One answer, and ' only one can be given to each of these vital questions, and that answer is, absolutely nothing ! Mr. Pierpoint says that " Lord Salisbury and his Administration have brought the Empire safely through all the difficulties and dangers that surrounded it, and ! have made England's power felt and respected through- out the world." This is grand phrastug but, unfortu- nately, it is not in accordance with facts. Every news- paper reader knows that the Boers are still opposing our I armies and slaying our soldiers ; that fire and sword are ! ! now trying to put down rebellien in Ashanti ; that the slaughter of the Arabs in the Soudan has not yet brought about the trade and tnissioning that were expected of it; and that the difficulty in China is not only not settled, but i actually becoming more menac- ing every day. The ex-member very discreetly abstains from solicit- ing our votes on social grounds, for he knows very well that questions affecting the real interests of the people do not, and never did,find much favour with legislators ,of the Salisbury and Chamberlain types. War is their forte, because by it they can rouse the baser passions of the people and thus divert their attention from their , wrongs and oppression at borne. The war in South Africa is the ground upon which Mr. Pierpoint seeks re-election to Parliament. OE that war he says, " I affirm it to have been a just war, and one for the origin of which we are in no way rcsponsib'e." I deny both these propositions. No war can be just, because the Creator has not yet made man the arbiter of human life. If a man under the influence of some sinister motive kills his neighbour we call his crime murder ; and what is the killing of thousands on the battle field for selfish interests but a multiplication of that crime ' Then with regard to reaoonsinility for the war, tho opinion of Mr. Pierpoint will not relieve us of our share of it. Both parties were to blame in the matter, and He to whom vengeance belongeth will hold each responsible for its full share of the awful bloodshedding in South Africa.

For these reasons I would earnest!y counsel my fellow workers t, withhold their votes from Mr. Pier- ppint• Tnat gentleman represents a party who have no love for the working classea except at election times when they condescend to use them for their political ends. A Government which has already sacrificed forty thousand men, and expended nearly one hundred mil- lions of their hard earnings in destroyint two little Republics is not one that should command the respect and support of working men wit) value their own inde- pendence and the freedom of their country. And, be- sides, let them remember that all the while the war in South Africa has hen going on thousands of their fellow-workers in India have been dying of starvation and plague, unpitied and unrelieved by the most power- ful and most boastful Tory Government of modern times.—Yours truly, lONA. 25th September, 1900.

SUICIDE OF A LYMM FARM LABOURER.

A Public Mortuary Wanted.

Mr. E. A. White, coroner, held an inquest ac the Court House, Lymm,on Manday,touching the death of Patrick Garrity, a farm .labourer, who was drowned in the Canal on Saturday. John Conellen, a labourer, said the body was that of Patrick Garrity, a farm labourer, who was about 40 years of age. He left him about 6.30 on Saturday night at the Nag's Head, Lymin. He was not drunk. He worked with him at Mr. Thomas Davis's, at High Leg,b, and had been driaking for about a fottnignt. He had been with deceased all the day. They had been in differ.nt peblic-houses. They had been drinking beer all day. He asked deceased to come home, but he said it was "too soon," and be left him. Edward Leather, farmer, Warburton, said that on Saturday night, about 10 10, he heard a voice saying: " Here goes for it," and tnen &splash. He gave an alarm, and a woman came wkth a light and a clothes prop, but he could not get heed of the body. A man afterwards dived for it, but failed to bring it up until they got grappling irons. Thomas Wrule, barman at the Bull's Head, Lymtn, depose] that he was informed the; a man was drowning in the Canal, and he dived in and got hold of the body, which was at the bottom, near the middle, but it was too heavy so he bad to leave go. Thomas Winstanley, landlord of the Nags's Head, Lymm, said he knew the deceased. He had called in his house several times during the last fortnight. Ha called about noon. and had several pints of beer, and left abont 7.30 the first, time, and came again about 9.30 but had nothing then. He left at 10.10. when he was sober but restless.

The Corouor said it was a very creditable perform- ance on the part of the man Boyle to jumpiato the canal and try and save the life of the deceased. A verdict of "Suicide by drowning whilst temporarily insane," was returned. Sergeant Garner asked the Coroner to call attention to the want of a public mortuary in Lymm and suggested that one should be built by the Lymm Urban District Council when they erected the new offices. The Coroner endorsed Sergeant Garner's view, and hoped the District Council would do sJmething in the matter at an early date. Toe jury also suggested that the Bridgewater Canal Company should place lifc-nuoys on each side of Lymm. Bridge, as such a number of accidents had occurred. Boyle was called into the ro -In and thanked for big brave conduct in jumping into the canal in his endeavour to save the life of the deceased.

COUNTY REVISION COURTS. PENKETEL

Mr. James Thomas Foard, revising barrister, attended at the Crows Rotel, P3nketh, on Tu. )sday morning to revise the lists of voters and county and parochial elec- tors for the townships of Bold, Cuerdley, Great Sankey, and Penketh. Mr Flogand Owen and Mr W Mawdsley appeared for the Conservatives and Mr El B Shores for the Liberals.—Tho only point of interest arose in regard to a claim for a lodger's vote in Great Sankey by Mr R Evans, solicitor, Warrington. Mr Shores contended that as the claimant made a gross payment for rooms and board at his father's house the claim was bad.—Mr Owen agreed acd remarked that the claim was not his.—Mr Shores repudiated the suggestion that he had put forward the claim.—The claim was disallowed.

===== 0002601_19000928_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON.

At the Warrington Police Station in the afternoon, Mr Feard revised the lists for Warringtln, Poulton-with- Fearnhead, Woolston-with-Vartinscroft, an Rixton- witirGlanbrook. Several ownership claims for War- rington were allowed. The whole of the business was quite formal. A court had been fixed for the evening.but by arrange- ment between the agents the business was completed at the afternoon sitting. NEWTON. _ _ _

A Court for the Revision (7)f-tne voters lists of Earles- town, Newton, Burtnnwood, Winwick-with-Hulme, Southworth-with-Croft, was held at the Newton Town Hall on Wednesth.y, by Mr J T Foard. Revising Barris- ter. Mr W Holland Owen, with Mr W Mawaley, appeared for the Conservatives, and Mr El B Snores for the Liberals. Very little interest appeared to be taken in the pncudings, and there was a small attendance of the public. A YEOMANRY VOLUNTEER LOSES HIS VOTE. The only case of any interest Wai in respect of the obj-ction by the Liberal agent to the name of Arthur Owbridge, whose name appeared on the list as for 301, Wargravc-r,ad,Earlestown. It app•ared that Owbridge is at present serving in South Africa with the Imperial Yeomanry, and hislather appeared to answer the ob- jection. In the course of the examination it turned nut that the father resided at 301, Wargrave-road, and the son also lived in Wargrave-road, but the house bore no number. Mr. Shores ssid that Arthur Owbridge had never claimed and had not been put on the register for the house occupied. Mr. Owbridge said his son did not occupy 301, Wet- grave-road, but he occupied another house, and during his absence in South Africa had allowed another to live in the house, but it was in their occupation yet. The Barrister : What do you mean by "our" Mr. Owbridge said when his son came back from the front, he had a right to walk into that house. and the objection to his son was wrong, becalm, it objected to 301, which was his father's house, and it was invalid. The Barrister said the point was that Arthur Ow- bridge did not re4ide at 301, Wargrave-road, and conse- cusently the entry in the register was wrong, and the objection must stand. There were no other cases of interest, but there were several passages at arms between the respective repro- sentatives on technical grounds. In the evening a sitting was held for the benefit of working men, but nothing of any moment transpired.

PRELI MINARY ADVBRTISIIMENT.

WARRINGTON PARLIAMENTARY ELECTION, 1900' Jthe UNDERSIGNED, being the appointed 1 Election Agent for Robert Pierpolnt, Esq.. a Candidate at the above Election, hereby give notice that, in view of the provisions of the Corrupt and Illegal Practices Prevention Act. 1883. the said Candidate will not be answerable or accountable for any payment for goods supplied, services rendered, or expenses incurred by any person acting or claiming to act, on his behalf, unless such purchase, service, or expense has been previously authorised in writing un,ler my hand, _

Signed. HENRY GREENALL. of No. 3, Winwiek-at., Warrington. Dated 2Cth September, 1900.

TO THE ELECTORS OF THE BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON.

GENTLEMEN.-

Her Majesty having dissolved Her fourteenth Parlia- ment, I venture, in response to the cordial invitation which I have received.to offer myself a third time as your representative.

During the past five years we have experienced as regards foreign affairs a most trying and difficult period, but Lord Salisbury and his Administration have brought the Empire safely through all the difficulties and dangers that surrounded it, and have made Eng!and's power felt ard respected throughout the world. I might win confidence refer to the many measures which have been passel by Iler Majesty's Government during the Parliament now ending, for the welfare of the country at large,and the working classes in particular, as well as to the extraordinary revival and prosperity which have occurred in connection with the Trade of the Country,butl feel it is in respect of the great war in which we have been engaged during the past 12 months that the verdict of the Electors is asked and must be given. This war provoked by the ambition and benighted ignorance of the Boer oligarchy, culminating in an invasion of British territory anti in the issuing of an insulting ultimaturnis now practically at an end. So far as we are concerned, I affirm it to have been a just war and one for the origin of which we are in no way responsible, and that there is reason to believe it will for ever convince the Boers or and manifest to the world at large, the determination of the English Nation to maintain their great Empire intact. I deplore the great loss of valuable lives that has occurred, but the devotion and self sacrifice of our Soldiers, and Sailors, and the ready response to the call to arms by Volunteers of all classes of Society and from all parts of the Kingdom, together with the enthusiastic assistance of our pmverfial colonies, have formed an object lesson to the world at large showing that the Kingdom and the Empire are one and indivisible.

I ask you for your votes and support at the approach- ing Election, as a pledge that you approve of the policy pursued by Lord Salisbury and his colleagues regarding the justice and necessity of the War, and that you desire that the complete and final settle neat of the South African question shall be left in their hands, so that there may be no repetition of weak surrender. and no risk run of there ever again arising a hostile State in that part of the Queen's dominions. Thus only may you feel assured that the sacrifice of life and treasure during the 'past year has not been in vain: and that the two former republics now inbsgral parts of the Queen's dominions, shill remain for ever under the Union Ja:.:k.

I am Gentlemen. Your faithful servant, ROI3IIIIT PIHRPOINT.

St. Austina. Warrington, 2lat September, 19)0.

THE EXAMINER.

SEPTEMBER 28Ta 1900.

THE hard hitting of the

Blow for Blow. Liberal leaders is new a marked feature of the cam- paign. The Government is getting blow for blow, and the effect is rather startling. The defence of the Government, mainly left to Mr. CHAMBER- LAIN and Mr. BALFOUR, gets weaker and weaker, and there are important daily secessions from the Unionist side. When the fight began it was appar- ently one for a compact united Cabinet. It is no longer possible to say anything of the kind. Mr. GOSOLIEN has retired from the Government, and there are ominous hints as to other changes. Lord SALISBURY and Sir M. HICKS-BEACH are likely to be affected by an important reduction in the Ministerial majority now admitted to be certain. There are clamourers for the retirement of Lordl LANSDOWNE, because of his incompetent manage- ment of the war. This is the best answer to Mr. WYNDHAM at Dever; and when he had been praising the War Office his I/ constituents reminded him of the obsolete guns in the forts of Dover, and he had to admit their existence. The effort to charge upon the late Government that it had neglected the army is met by a quotation from Mr. BALFOUR in 1896, when he said: "There never was a moment, I believe, in the recent history of this country when the British Empire was a better fighting machine than it is at the present moment." The other night Mr. BALFOUR had the audacity to say that the Liberals had achieved no great roform in the Army since 1870. The retirement of the DUKE of CAMBRIDGE and the acceptance of the suggestions of the Hartington Commission for a Commander- in-Chief selected for a term, was the last official act of the late Liberal Government. The war in South Africa found us unprepared. The Boors had been busy, and no remonstrance was offered. We could not remonstrate nor check, Mr. BAL- FOUR said, because the Raid had shut our mouths. He went further, because he said some time ago: "'We entered on this war insufficiently prepared," and peace nego- tiations would have been hampered by warlike preparations. Mr. CHAMBERLAIN goes on repeat- ing the "barefaced inaccuracy" (to use Lord SPENCER'S phrase) about his negotiations being rendered null and void by certain letters, to which Mr. KRUGER paid more attention than to anythiug else. But it was the Raid, and the South African Committee fiasco, which had more to do with failure than anything else. Mr. CHAMBERLAIN is astute enough to forget the two things, and to prefer to libel his own countrymen. He said at Oldham: "I am here to fight for the flag." Well, the blow in reply is that we only tarnished it by beginning a war without due preparation, and that Colenso and Magersfontein are as bitter as Majuba. The flag has been fought for by our soldiers, and the fight is nearly over, and we all joice in the fact. It is to soil the flag to bring it into party conflicts,and to claim it as a Unionist emblem. Mr. BALFOUR says the country is only to peak about one inatter—the settlement. Sir W. HARCOURT has properly styled this a" Constitu- tional impertinence," an impudent declaration. The country has to review the whole conduct of an Administration which appeals to it for a new lease of power. It would be an absurdity for any other course to be taken. What the country sees is that Mr. CHAMBERLAIN and Mr. BALFOUR are fighting for the future Premiership under cover of the war, and that the Government to which they belong tells us othing about what they will do for domestic affairs. Will they appeal again when South Africa has been settled ? Noc they. Hence the chatter about" a clear cut issue" is mere balderdash. The issue is disguised by a trick, and it is this trick which is producing Liberal unity and Liberal enthusiasm in increasing volume every day. The British Colonies are what they are by reasan of Liberal reforms and statesman- ship. South Africa was peaceful and its races united in 1895. Now the Unionists claim to have created the loyalty that is the fruit of self-rule ; and having unsettled the peace that Liberals had secured, they denounce the Liberals as incapable of settling South Africa! Such Brummagem fallacies are, happily, being found out.

• LORD LANSDOWNE has been Warform Office compelled to reply to LORD Re. ROSEBERY. The DUKE of DEVONSHIRE had previously found himself in the same fix. The head of the War Office, however, has fared no better than the Chairman of the Defence Committee and the President of the Council. MR. BRODRICK and others have accused the late Liberal Government of neglecting the army, and it is established that the worst charge—

that of reducing the artillery—is wholly untrue, because the reduction was made by MR STANHOPE a Unionist SLicretary for:War. LORD LANSDOWNE now gives his testimony to another point. On coming into office, ho says "the present Ministry found in existence a scheme of War Office reorganisation which the outgoing Secretary for War, SIR HENRY CAMPBELL-BANNERMAN, cer- tainly intended to bring forward, I presume with the concurrence of his chief." This scheme, "with comparatively slight alteration," was adopted by the present Government. But for these changes remarks LORD LANSDOWNE, "it would have been impossible to place and maintain in the field the great army which has for the last few months fought for the Empire in South Africa." This is excellent testimony to Liberal policy, drawn out, by the way, in defence of the present Government. It took a Liberal scheme, and under it the war has been conducted. But there is to be a Parliamen- tary Committee to inquire into the conduct of the War Office under this scheme, and hence it may be assumed that errors of administration have occurred in spite of an excellent plan. LordRosE- BURY'S charge was that "administrative reform could not safely be entrusted to those who appoin- ted, conducted, and ignored the Hartington Com- mission." The sting of the passage is in the word "ignored." Lord ROSEBERY is sure to reply that the contention now is that the reforms made in the War Office were only, as Lord LANSDOWNE admits, founded upon the report of the Commission." Portions of the recommendations of the Commis- sion were certainly "ignored," and Lord ROSEBERY will be able to point to them, and the jaunty way in which they were set aside. There was, as Lord ROSEBERY contends, "want of military foresight and preparation." The blame rests upon the War Office. It cannot rest anywhere else. The CHAN- CELLOR of the EXCHEQUER,On behalf of the Treasury, has repelled the suggestion that he hampered the War Office. Accordingly, we come back to what is believed to be Lord ROSEBERY'S charge, namely administrative inefficiency, ignorance,and want of preparation. How can the country trust the pre- sent Government to put the army into a bettor state to meet Imperial necessities when it has made so many blunders in conducting the present war, with such ample financial resources at its command ? Administrative efficiency covers more than the mechanical re-arrangements to which Lord LANSDOWNE would limit it. The war was entered upon with ignorance as to the artillery the Boers could put in the field, the extent of the Boor resources in men, the foreign contingents, the intimate alliance with the Free State, the re- sources of the Free State, and with poor maps and appliances. It was thought that 70,000 men would be ample to meet the Boers, and that small moan - tam n guns from India and out-of-date field artillery,would suffice. Can we repose confidence in men, political and permanent, who made such serioui mistakes? Their grasp of circumstances, their official industry, their professional capacity are unequal to the great task the conntry wants doing. Lord ROSEBERY will therefore have na difficulty in making good his main contentionst and the electors must see that if we are to have effective reforms at the War Office and in our military system, we shall not obtain them from the present Government unless a strong Liberal Opposition is elected to watch over the work, avert weakness, ar-1 prevent half measures.

Here is a strange story of a still stranger boor reported to have occurred at Warrinaton• hold the " Weekly Telegraph " responsible for It was as follows :—" When the news of tho Battles of Waterloo arrived at Warrington—then, as "vt O„, a thriving place—the chief magistrate of the wl' declared that he should add to the projected illu- minations by burning his own house down, and he actually did so. It seems that the house WaS old one, mostly wood, and that his worship he' previously contemplated dismantling it and build- ing a new one. But his example was contagious,and the owner of a considerable block of what would in these days be called " slum" property happening to be a political rival of the other incendiary,bura the ancient tenements as a counter-attraction. Un- fortunately, a big house not contemplated in the scheme took fire also, but the crowd and the ail- thorities, thinking that it was all part of the shoo', did nothing to put this fire out, and even res- trained the pwner as a mad drunkard when /I, danced aboet exhorting them to put the fire out. Can any of our readers vouch for the authenticitY of these strange happenings ?

IThough the heat wave promised us in Septet!'" ber has fallen short of expectation in some degrees and the weather still leaves us at liberty te breathe, they are having on the other side of tha Atlantic a spell of heat beyond precedent. Wl° gather this not from any formal news received over the cables, but from a resolution which appear 3, duly recorded in the journal of the proceedings 0' the Kentucky legislature. MATTERS OF MOMENT. ---- A Provincial Letter. - •

Floodgates of oratory have been opened in all parts of the provinces during the week; but it can by no means be said that there has been any great or general display of political enthusiasm over the General Election hitherto, Indeed, in these days of record making, and record breaking, a record may be said to have been established in the shape of the Very tamest Election of the century. Candidates on both sides have been enlarging upon" the issues before the country," knowing, as every newspaper reader knows, that practically there is bat one issue, and that it is the question now whether Liberals or Unionists shall have the power to deal 'with and administer the affairs of the annexed terri- tory in South Africa. On this point the whole Elec- tion will turn.

Of all subjects that have to be dealt with by whatever Government is chosen, the first and fore- most is that of the reform of the war-)ffice and the strengthening of the national defences. Yet strange to say, this question does not appear to have, found its proper place in any of the speeches of the candidates. After the excitement and in- terest tha !has been taken in the campaign. it is a mean ince feet that does not fully understand the vari9us bearings of the matter, and as men seem to take a de:ight in still airing their views upon South Africa, candidates are tempted to pander to the craving. But there is something far more im- portant now than the justice or otherwise of the war, or the settlement in the Transvaal or the Orange River Colony; we have not as yet been in- formed what the nature of the war-office reforms is to be, or what steps are contemplated to put the army on a proper footing.

It is reported that there is no class among the etay-at-homes mlre badly " hit " by the War in South Africi than the professional book-makers. During the past twelve months their business has experienced such a "slump "as will not be easily get over by some of them. Their clients are mainly men of means to whom life would be considered very dull without some special excitement such as is obtained in the piping times of peace from the arena of competitive sport, and from that alone. During the past year, however, the excitement to be derived from campaigning on the South African Veldt has proved an irresistible counter-attraction for a considerable number of the bookmakers' wealthy patrons; and how great a difference this has made to the business may be gathered from recent proceedings in the London Bankruptcy Court.

Mayor Craigie's annual report on agricultural education contains much interesting information. It is not long since the value of instruction in agricultural matters was recogniz3d and the work placed on an organized tooting, with a Minister of agriculture at the head, An efficient and economi- cal means of systematising the various local schemes of technical instruction in this department bas now been found; and in several instances the aided centres are entrusted with complete farms, either hired directly by the college authorities, Which receive grants from the Board, or acquired by local authorities for educational and experi- inental purposes. Dairying has also been benefited and attention has even been given to cider making. On the whole, the report is highly satisfactory,and it can no longer be said that agriculture is neglect-.d industry.

There is reason t 3 believe that there is only too much truth in a statement made to the magistrates of the Weston-iuper-fare petty sessional division on Friday by Mr. if. H. Gregory, of the Western Circuit, to the effect that in almost every case in- augurated by a public body a technical objection arose, and that "in nine cases o'lt of ten the telhni- cal objection succeeded." It did 80 in the case before the bench, defended by Mr Gregory. It was an ordinary sun blind prosecution, in which the prin- cipal witness, as Inspector of Nuisances under the Urban District Conncil, had taken out a summons for the wrong date. The defendants are the largest tradesmen in the town, and use a considerable length of sun blinds in two thoroughfares ; so that the matter was of some public importance, but fell through on the objection mentioned. It is,perhars, too much to expect that other public bodies will condescend to either notice Mr Gregory's comment, or to accept a hint from the result of this and other similar cases. The pnblic have to pay,and they are known to be long suffering.

Though there has been a steady increase in the quantity of spirits prodnced since 1880, in Eng- land_and Ireland, the number of working distilleries bag during the interval remained practically stationary. But it is otherwise in the North. The number of distilleries in Scotland twenty years since was 121 ; and now the number is 161, the increase daring the past five years being 29. Argyll, a county, heads the list, with 33: Banffshira is second, with 25 ; and Morayshire third, with 16. The counties of Aberdeen and Inverness have ten each, and Ross and Cromarty have eight. In the north and nortt-mist, ot Scotland there are alto- gether eighty distilleries.

If the Revising Barrister in the Isle of Wight has correctly interpreted the law, many soldiers tow in South Africa will suffer a grave injustice. Ile has struck off the register the names of some seventy men of the Ist Rifle Brigade, who were stationed at Parkhurst before the war, and are now serving at the front. The ground ot objection was that they hal be3n absent from the country for raore than four months, and this was held by the Barrister to be a fatal objection. The anomalies of the registration law are numerous, and this adds te their number. The votes of Volunteers and Yeomen serving in South Africa have been protected by special enactment, and it has an ungenerous look,to say the least, to distinguish between the regular and auxiliary branches of the service by depriving the regular soldiers of the franchise.

The Inebriates Act 1898 continues to be almost a dead letter. The Parliamentary paper just issued, dealing with its working, contains little of provin- cial interest. The defect of the reformatories already established by private philanthropic or public contributions, and licensed under the Act, is that the management appear to have no effective means of summarily dealing with the violent inebriates who may be committed to the institution. It is possibly safe to lay down as a general proposi- tion that an inebriate when placed beyond the reach of alcoholism loses any tendency to violent acts. There are, however, some people who are vicious and ungovernable whether drunk or sober, and it is for the control of these people that. special means are desired at Brentry, and other -8 imilar places,

If any elector has not yet decided how to vote, there is one elementary matter he should keep in view. One member is, of course, a mere unit in a souse composed of 6 per cent, statesmen and Politicians. lie may make promises at the hust- ings, but one man alone can do nothing. All that It is in his power to do as an individual is to press 'Upon the Government some particular subject, 'which he considers is for the well-being of his country. Therefore, it is important that every can- didate should let it be fully known before going to the poll what he is prepared to undertake if re- turned to Parliament; and it is for the elector by questioning him, to see that he does so.

In the provinces there is a marked uncon- cern, generally speaking as to the issues of this epoch-making election. It is very Strange when One Comes to think of Sir lienry Campbell-Bannerman only putting in an appearance at Stirling on Wednesday. more than a week after the decision of the Privy Council had been announced and of how in Gladstonian days the Old chief would long before have carried the torch of his Lurning eloquence up and down the country. . Henry's "friendly lead "could not be read until the day before yesterday, after all his leading °Pnonents had spoken. except Lord Salisbury. The Liberal leader was announced as being in London Ott Saturday. The delay between then and Wed- nesday would not have mattered so much on some occasions, but must have proved somewhat orobarra-sing to certain of the Liberal candidetes 'waiting for their" cue," It was well in some res- recta that Mr Asquith had already spoken.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES. THE DANGER. OF " BOLTING- FJOD:'—A. Luns BOY'S DEA.TII,

At Runcorn Police Court on •Cuesday afternoon Mr. White, deputy coroner, held an inquest on the body of Patrick Dalton, aged eight year, who died suddenly on Monday.—Margaret Dalton, wife of James Dalton, dock labourer. of 6, Church View, said the deceased wai her son. He had been sub- ject to fits since he was two years old. Lately be bad not been very well and on Sunday morning he refused his food, complained of being ill and she put him to bed. Afterwards he had some bread, bacon and tea and subsequently appeared to be all right and played throughout the day. Re ate a very good dinner and tea and want to bed at nine o'clock. On Monday morning he was very sick and later he again vomited. A neighbour went for Dr. Robinson, who, on arrival, a few minutes later declared the boy to be in a state of collapse.He died at noon.—Dr. Delaney deposed that he had made a p 056 mortern examination of the body. He said the food he found in the stomach appeared not to have been masticated, but to have been bolted. The lining membrane of the stomach was inflamed. This might have been caused by undigested food.— In his opinion death was due to infl xmmation of the stomach. The boy was of delicate constitu- tion.—The jury returned a verdict in accordance with the medical testimony.

MARRIAGE OF MR. R. W. RUSHTON'.

On Wednesday afternoon a large gathering assembled in Victoria Park Wesleyan Church, Wavertree, Liverpool, the occasion being the marriage of Mr. Robert W Rushton, of Greenway- road, Runcorn, to Miss Kate Annie Agnes (Kitty) Greaves, of Sefton Park, Liverpool. The ceremony was performed by the Rev. Walter Charlesworth. resident minister, special music being rendered by Mr. Edgar Deyes, the organist of the church. The bride was given away by Mr. John Conies, ot Run- corn. Toe bridesmaids were Miss Ada Greaves, sister of the bride. and Miss Harriet Bennett Rush- ton sister of the bridegroom. Mr. Walter Peacock acted as best man. and Mr John Bebbington as groomsman. After the ceremony the wedding break- fast was served at the residence of Mrs B. Spearing, Portman-road, Sefton Park. The happy couple were the recipients of numerous handsome presents. They received many hearty clngratulations, and left Lime-street Station at four o'clock en route for Colwyn Bay, to spend the honeymoon. RUNCORN HARRIERS. The athletic season being over, the body of runners who chase the scraps of paper o'er field and fallow are now looking forward to the opening run of the cross-lountry season, which will take place from the Coffee House Hotel, Wootton, under the auspices of the Liverpool and District Cross- Country Association. In view of this the Runcorn Harriers held a team race on Saturday, from the Bull's Head, when a good number of runners put in an appearance. Toe secretary and captain se- lected their teams, and a start was made about 4 o'clock. The distance covered was about 8 miles in the direction of Rock Savage and Sutton Weaver and round home by Halton. A mile break was formed at Halton Brook, which proved most inter- esting, seeing that there was a lot of new blood competing. A close race was witnessed between Lewis, Ray, Hopkins and Thomas for first home, Lewis putting in a good finish, and winning a hard fought race. The following was the position of the teams ;—Secretary's team— 1 Lewis, 2 Ray, 3 Hopkins, 6 Stubbs; total 12 points. Captain's team :-4 Matthews, 5 Stringer, 7 Bignall, 8 Thomas; totalling 24 points, being a win for secretPry's team by 12 points. DEATH OF THE APPARITOR OF RUNCORN PARISH

===== 0002601_19000928_art0060.txt | included=true | included =====
CIEURCH,

We regret to announce the death of Mr. Samuel Hoxworth, the apparitor of Runcorn Parish Church which took Place at his residence, No. 1, Bridge- water-street, in the early hours of Sunday morning. Deceased had been the victim of paralysis for the last two years, and for seven weeks prior to his death he was confined to his bed. The end, there- fore was not unexpscted,and he weed away peace- fully shortly after midnight on Saturday He had held the office of apparitor at the Parish Church for the last eleven years. and the discharge of his duties was characterized by a genial and courteous bearing which made him popular with clergy, officials and congregation alike. For a period of thirty years he was a bellringAr at the church, and was connected with the Chester Diocesan Guild of Bellringers and tbe Lancashire Association of Bell- ringers. He was a member of the local branch of the Order of Rechabites, having held the office of trustee for a considerable period. Aithough not a native of the town, the late Mr. Hoxworth could olaim to be a Rut' cornian in every sense of the word. He wa's born in Warrington, but his parents came to Runcorn when he had attained his first year,and thus practically the whole of his life was spent in the town. He was present at the laying of the foundation stone of the present Parish Church in 1847, and could relate many interesting events in the history of the town. He leaves a widow and daughter to mourn his loss. The interment is fixed for Wednesday next at Runcorn Cemetery, prior to which there will be a choral service at the Parish Church.—The funeral took place on Wednesday at the Parish churchyard, when the Rev Canon Wood officiated. The whole of the clergy of the parish were represented, and the surpliced choir were also present to honour the deceased's memory. The attendance was large and included a number of the Union Tent of Rechabites, of which lodge the de- ceased was the oldest member.

RUNCORITI NO-1 ES. [By a Correspondent.]

The whole electioneerina machinery of the Northwich Division is now undoubtedly engaged at full pressure, and the nomination has been fixed for the sth and the polling for the 13th of October. The latter day is a SAturday,and as usual the results will not come to hand until the Monday. Addresses have been issued by both the candidates and each can be fairly said to have the "gloves on."

By the tactics of the Tory side (locally at any rate) it would, however, appear that the fight will include a round or two of " hitting below the belt" with the gloves discarded. As Sir John Brunner puts it, "it is a shame" that an old friend of the County like himself " should have to descend to the level of answering lying, and dirt throwing," instead of the great political issues of the times being openly and fear- lessly discussed. His speech, wain appears else- where, is a dignified if somewhat severe denuncia- tion of a bath of malicous aspersions. That the Tories know the North wich Division to be Radical and unassailable is self-evident, and Probably they hope to score by descending to baser issues than the discussion of tair and above board politics. Indeed, it is common knowledge that the Conservative Government have made mistakes, and have just closed a Parliament void of any lasting home legis- lature, and they therefore cannot face the music to that tune. Their hope of success lies in throwing np sufficient dust to blind the eyes of all to their shortcomings.

Locally the same thing prevails. Sir John Bruener is being attacked in his personal charac- ter, and rightly le appeals to his constituents to defend that character" as they would the charac- ter of a dearly beloved father or brother." I feel here again the Tories have made a mistake, for by descending to so low a degree they become con- temptible in the eyes of all free-born Britishers, and when those Britishers are chiefly of the Radi- cal persuasion, the Tories will find they will defeat their own ends. To Runcorn especially has Sir John Brunner endeered himself, and if the issue between him and Mr Samson is to be one of character, and an estimation of that character, there can be but one result.

Our local postmaster Mr. Okell, whom a few weeks ago we intimated had been promoted to a much more important past in Cornwall,l now learn has declined that post, but in recognition of his services he has been offt3red and accepted the post- mastership of Pontrefact. The post is of an equal value as the one originally offered him in Cornwall Mr. Okell leaves on Saturday, so I understand, His loss to the town will be greatly felt, for a more painstaking public official there never was,

THE DAIRIES AND COWSHEDS REGULATIONS.

ACTS ABOUT SCOTCH WHISKY. The consumption of Whisky in the United Kingdom is so general at the present time that ant' information with regard to its manufacture must be of interest. Highland Malt Whisky- is a simple distillation from Malt, made from Barley carefully dried in the Malt Kiln and partakes, therefore, of the pleasant flavour peculiar to dried Malt. The finest Barley only is used in making Malt for the distillation of this Whisky, and in its pu:- chase keen competition always ex sts. Considerable quantities of cheap Grain Spirit are,how- ever, made from MVZ3. foreign Barley and Unmalted Grain, which, on account of their lower prile, are used to blend with Malt Wh.sky in order to produce a cheaper article.

Without wishing to disparage the merits of Grain Spirit, it is not " Highland Malt Whisky." Grain Spirit may, to tie taste of some consumers, have merits of its own ; but if so. they need not be hidden under the title of" Highland Malt" Wh'sky, but the article should stand by itself and be sold under its proper designation of Grain Spirit. Fr this reason, no doubt, the public, with knowledge and taste, having once appreciated the flavour of a Malt Whisky, will not find anything in blends composed partly of Grain Spirit to recommend them.

Highland Malt Whisky, therefore, esoecially with the 'guarantee of the distiller, is and will tncome more and more the article sought after by the consurtier. W and A Gilbey, holding these views with regard to the highPr merits of Highland Whisky distilled oily from the best Malt, have used nothing, else in the brands offered by them to the public for the past 40 years, and at both their own Distilleries in the Glenlivet district— viz" GlPn-Spey " and " Strathmill "—the Whiskies are made entirely from Malted Barley grown in the United Kingdom. These two celebrated Distilleries are situated in the best barley districts in Scotland, and the supply of water, of which a great study is made in the distilling of Whisky, is of the purest quality, and ensures a fine and delicate flavoured article of concumotion.

W & Gilbey have 12,000 Butts of-Scotch Whisky maturing in El,r Majesty's Bonded VY'arehous.s, equal to over 1,600,000 2allons at proof streogth, and are thus enabled by their large stocks to maintain a thoroughly matured article, and a uaiform standard of 11+0 quality under their various brands, all of which are guaranteed iistilled from Pure Malt.

NOTES BY THE WAY AT EARLESTOWN.

[By A CORRESPONDENT.]

It is now settled that there will not be a Parlia- mentary contest in the Newton Division. Colonel Pilkington will therefore be denied the pleasure of a fight, and doubtless this fact will relieve him of a considerable amount of anxiety. Everything points to the fact that he would have been elected had a ballot been taken, but he must not think that because there is no contest, that the electors are unanimously in his favour. Were he to do so, he would be assuming a false position. A very great many people iii Earlestown have not forgotten Colonel Pilkington's action on the half-timer's que- tion, and had there been a strong and active Liberal Association in the Division, it would have gone hard with him on that question alone. Colonel Ptlkington may be a devoted Imperialist, but there are other matters affecting the welfare of this neighbourhood, besides warlike topics. Colonel Pilkington's election address has not up to the time of writing been issued, but a perusal of his speech at Earlestown on Monday night will give the electors a fair idea of his line of action, lie was great on the Workmen's Compensation Act, and he ventured the statement that whereas under the old Act compensation could cot be obtained without litigation, under the pre,ent measure there would be little or no litigation. I don't know upon what data the Colonel relies for his information, but I think if he will make enquiry, he will find there is quite as much, if not more, litigation now than ever there Wag before, and it does not always result to the benefit of the workmen. Of course this is outside large w)rks whicti have made pi.-- vision to :meet such cases without the aid of the law courts.

At the same meeting, Mr. Borron as Chairman made an egregious blunderin my opinion. Colonel Pfikington was posing as a candidate for the New- ton Division and was addressing the electors of that Division. A gentlemen at the close of the meeting asked for a reply to a question which he had put in writing and handed to the Chair- man. He was told, however, that he had no right to come there and ask questions seeing that he lived in the Newton Polling Di trier, but should wait until a meeting was held in his district, when he would have an opportunity of putting his question. What the question was matters not, but seeing that the gentleman was a voter in the Newton division, he had,in my opinion, a perfect right to a hearing as any meeting held in the division no matter what polling district he re- sided in. It, does no good to a cause to snuff out electors in this fashion, but every facility s:lould te given to them to elicit from the candidate answers on subjects in which they are interested, especially when they are backed up by a body,such as that which the questioner referred to represents It should be to Colonel Pilkington's interest to pro- pitiate all parties. Conservatives, it is well known, however, do not like questions at election times.

===== 0002601_19000928_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
FOOTBALL.

The game between Earlestown and Havdock on Saturday on the latter's ground was devoid of in- terest and certainly of excitement. It was too much one aided, and the flaydock men were unable to make any headway against the clever tactics of Bartley, and the sterling defence of Cibborn. Earlestown tried a new man named Walker at cen- tre forward, but he does not coma up to expecta- tion, and he has been dropped this week.

The visitors to the Earlestown ground on Satur- day are Southport Central, but the homesters must understand that if they intend to win they will have to put their shoulder to the wheel. There is a slight re-arrangement of the team, Whitehouse going centre forward in place of the new man Walker. They should just about win, but as South- port are equal in record with Earlestowu no chances will have to be given. A win would place Earles- town well up on the League table.

There were no matches in the neighbourhood on Saturday.

Hush!

Mr. Brodrick, M.P. (the Tory Under-Secretary for Foreign Affairs declared (at Guildford on August 29th) that he could not discuss .he question of guns with Cap- tain Larnoton " pending the inquiry which has been promised into the conduct of the war." Ministers are to rean the benefits of Lord Roberts's strategy and Tommy Atkins's valour, but when Captain Lambton (with the best reasons for knowing) complains of the' ridiculous toys" of gunr, Mr. B:odrick says : " Hush ! you mustn't menticn that—it's going to be inquired into." For cool impertinence tnis would be hard to beat. Ministers are first of all to be given a further term of office and then to have their fitness for that office inquired into.

DOESN'T THIS LOOK AS IF TORY MINISTERS

WERE AFRAID TO HAVE HIE FACI'S KNOWN ?

Why have I dissolved Parlia- ment in September.

(1) Because I have alwa'A hated an extended Franchise, and by dissol7ing in September I secure that nobody who has come to live in a Constituency since July. 1898, shall have a Vote. I thus deprive tens of thonsands of Work- ing Men of their rights as citizens. And serve them right too ! Why cannot they go on living in the same place ? I am always to be heard oZ at flatfiell (2) Because Every day sick and wounded men are return ing home from South Africa with shocking tales of the Conduct of the War by the War OffiC9, of the Imbecility of the arrangements, of the improper arma• meats, of the lack of food, of the breakdown of the Medical Department ; and of many other matters, all o f which mast acme day aoo:i be investigated hy a com- mission. What a fool I ,should ho to wait until the whole truth is known ! The fact is, I have already waited too long.

(3) Because lam able in September to hide behind Lend Roberts and his brave men, still risking their lives in S.uth Africa; and under cover of their popularity and martial exploits I and my Lansdownes and Chaplins may hope once more to sneak into office. Were Ito wait till the War was over I should not be able to do this, but might have to defend Chamberlain s diplomacy before the War and Lansdowne's incompetency after it. I was never much of a Jingo myself but I have no diffi- culty in recognising that " Brava Old Bobs " is better electioneering than" Vote for Salisbury and Chamber- lain." J wonder what my old enemy,Lord Beaconsfieldt would think of me now ; but regrets are vain. Still, it is in my heart to wish that Joe had remained a Radical. He cklled me a Homicide once. He could hardly do so a cal n. (4) Because

Whilst the war is waging, the country will not be disposed to call my Government to acccunt for its sins of Omission and of C9mmissian. All that sickening nonsense about Old Age Pensions will be for- gotten. Arthur's ridiculous, not to say dishonest election card at Manchester would be too trivial a thing even to mention. My does to myself and the other Land lords of England, large as they were, are mere 11A-bites when we are spending a hundred millions of pounds ster- ling on crushing the quasi-independence of:a lot of psalm singing Dutch farmers. The rottenness of poor Sir Michael's finance, the grotesque absurdities of Chaplin, the repeated failures of Ritchie, the deplorable weakness of Ridley, the unveiled disloyalty of Gorst, the growing infirmities of my nephew's temper, and the ill-concealed ambition of Chamberlain to shove the whole lot on one side and to taka the stage himself as Actor Manager, are dangerms subjects were thev to be freely handed, as they would be if I miited till the war was over. 'Therefore I will wait no longer.

EARLESTOWN 8c NEWTON.

BOY DROWNED AT NEWTON

On Saturday afternoon, a boy, 6 years of age, named Frank Ellam was drowned in Newton Lake. He with two or three other boys west to the Lake and commenced to wade in the water. Ellam ven— tured too far into the water and was suddenly immersed, and he was drowned in full view of his compasions. John Hassall 5 years old,who was with. the deceased,on rain commencing to fall went home, and from enquiry it was elicited that Ellam was in the lake. Dragging operations took place, and the bpdy was recovered two hours after irnmersioin lying in a depth of 4 or 5 feet of water, 10 yards from the side. A verdict of "Accidental death" was returned. PICTURE COMPOSITION.

At the last meeting of tha Earlestown and New- ton Photographic Society held at the headquarters "The Art PrJ•ss " 25, Bridge-street, a paper on " Picture Composition " was admirably given by Mr. _Ronald King, dealing with the knowledge and use of nature, rules of composition, etc. He also, showed a number of examples of light and shade. The meetings of the above socisty are held fort- nightly on Thursday evenings at 8p m. The next meeting being October 4th, the society will be glad to welcome any new member.—Mr. W. R. Shocking Accident to a Warrington Carter. On Wednesday morning at five o'clock, Patrick Monaghan, agei 38, Anne-street, Warrington, met with very serious injuries. He was driving a furniture van, and when at Acton Bridge near North wich, the hor,e knocked him down. He lay in the road a long time before be was seen. Dr. Smith afterwards attended him, finding a compound fracture of the leg and injuries to the facc. He was removed to the Victoria Infirmary, Northwich The Mayoralty.

A caucus meeting of the members of the Town Council was held on Monday afternoon, at the Town Hall,to select the Mayor for the ensuing year. There was a good attendance,over which Alderman Sutton presided. On the proposition of the Chairman, seconded by Councillor Plumpton,and supported by Aldermen Fraucomb and Heaton, it was decided to usk Alderman Roberts to allow himself to be nomin— ated for office a second year,each member referring to the able and impartial manner in which his Wor— ship had discharged his duties during the past twelve months. A deputation, consisting of the gentlemen above named, together with Councillor Pierpoint, were appointed to wait upon the Mayor and inform him of the decision of the meeting. They did so,and his Worship intimated his willingness to accept office again.

THE AUTHOR.

The author of our new story, ALICE MURIEL WILLIAMSON. has made a great reputation among the younker generation of novelists. Barn in 1869 at one of the Livingstone Manor Houses on the Hudson lifyer,for she was a Miss Livingstone,she commenced to weave romances before she could hardly write. Of her very youthful efforts none has ever seen the light in print, but when she was only fifteen her first story was published. liven then she did not embark upon the profession of literature seriously. NORTHERN UNION.

LANCASHIRE SENIOR COMPETITION.

Warrington's First Defeat. Stockport ieflicced the first reverse on Warrington this season, at Edgeley Park last Saturday, after a very hard game. The visitors were without two valuable players in Fish an i Taylor, substitutes being found in Duck- worth and Jolley. Stockport turned out as selected and placed a strong team in the field. The weather was fine but dull, and the ground in good order. while there would be quite 4,000 people round the enclosure to wit- ness what is always a popular fixture at Stockport. Mr. S. Williams of Salford was referee. and a few minutes after the advertised time the teams lined out as follow : Warrington :—Hallam, -full back, Duckworth, Isher- wood, Lawless and Hockenhali, toree quarters, Bate and Burgess, half backs, B ardman, Mereweather, Eden, Scholtz2. Swift, Morrison. Jolley and Andrews, forwards. Stockport : J Smith, full back; Hodgson, Crocker, Worsley, and Robinson, tbreequarters ; Bevan and Williams, half backs; Garside, Oram, Owen, Burrows, Trotter, Simister, Taylor,ancl Griffitles,for wards. Stockport lost the toss. and Dr. Hillier, the Conserva- tive candidate kicked off for them, Scholtze returning to centre where Boardman got in the wars and was tem- porarily laid out. A smart punt by Lawless gave War- rington a footing in front or the home goal, Duckworth and Isherwood gaining furtner ground by neat passing. Hallam had a fut le shot at goal from a penalty, Bevan marking. Then the homesters came away with a fine dribble headed by Trotter and Burrows, Hocken- hall dropping pluckily on the ball near the Warrington line. Duckworth next intercepted a pass and threw to Isherwood who broke away and finally punted in touch. Worsley had a drep from Hallam's kick but sent wide. Warrington rushed back to the borne half, Morrison kicking over the line. Mereweather dashed up and threw himself at the ball, but just missed his mark and hurt himself with the effort. He resumed after a short absence, but was never the " same man" for the rest of the game. Stockport assumed the upper hand again and drove Warrington to the line. Duckworth kicked out feebly and Worsley smartly threw to Robinson who dashed over:at the corner, no goal resulting. This reverse aroused Warrington and a)me smart play caused the home team to defend, but they presently attacked &pin and Simister breaking away from a forward rush an strongly and passed to Robinson,ths wing man:finisb- ing up behind the posts. Eleven added a goal, and Warrington found themselves 8 points behind. They rallied again, however, and a fine movement between Bate, Isherwood, and Duckworth ended in the latter scoring far out, Hallam just missing with a place-kick. Lawless had hard luck just before the interval with a knock on when he. had an open field. Half-time: Stockport, 8 points ; Warrington, 3. Swift recommenced hostilities, and even play ensued both ends being visited in turn. Hallam was frequently called upon but responded splendidly. Robinson was twice dangerous, once being grassed near the line and the other time grounding behind the goal after a bad knock-forward. VVors'ey made a fine run for the borne- team and passed to 'lrotter, the latter being 'promptly bowled in touch. During a spell of attacking at the other end Warrington were very near scoring. Bate made a dive for the line and dropped short. while Law- less and Hockenhall missed a chance through slow pass- ing. There was no further scoring and Stockport won by 8 points to 3 points.

St. Helens Well Beaten at Home.

Runcorn have often triumphed over St. Helens, but so far this season their form had not been too brilliant. They had, evidently not started with all their best payers. but the severe defeat at Swinton compelled them to set their house in order,with satisfactory results. The team whica was at St. Helens on Saturday could scarcely be improred upon. Although playing on a wet ground with a greasy ball, the men hardly ever made a mistake, and their catching, kicking, and passing was as clever and accurate as would have been possible under the best conditions. There was a decidea contrast in the play of their opponents. With the exception of Traynor, the threenuarters on the St. Helens side could scarcely Oro anything right, missing catches and passes, and kicking with bad ju lament when they got the ball. The game wae started by Mr. Henry Seton-Karr, the member for the borou4h, who was looking up his car,- stituents in view of the General Election. tie did not lead them to victory ou this occasion, their unbeaten eartific4te being very badly smudged. They had a chance just after the start, when Butterworth and Houghton were in difficulties near their line,but Doherty failed to give Whiteley a clear opening. and away Run- corn went to the other end. The Runcorn halves were making plenty of openings for their backs, but the tackling of the " Saints " kept Wilkinson and Jones from scJring. Toe most dangerous attack was really made by Houghton, who followed up one of his kicks with such smartness that Foultes only just managed to kick dead in time. Relief was cleverly gained b 7 Traynor by a rare dash under his own poste,and his kick to Houghton being mulled, Whiteley and Melvin should have walked in had they had the presenc3 of mind to put their feet to the ball. Then Runcorn took up a position in front of the S. Helens' goal, mainly through a misunderstanding by Foulkes and Traynor, and the ball being passed out by Richardson, via Faulkner and Butterworth, to Jon-s, the latter scored easily. Farmer missed the goal. The superiority of Runcorn was more than ever manifest when ends were changed. The wind was rather in their favour, and their long kicks repelled every invasion, and placed the "Saints" on the d ,fence. Once they had a beautiful opening, but lost their heads. Houghton had made one of his few mistakes, and a couple of the St. Helens forwards bad only to go straight for the line, but the chance was muddled, and eventually lost. Runcorn returned to the attack, and Doherty fumbled under the posts, and Whitley Failing to kick dead whilst he had plenty of time, saw Messenger sprint up and ground the ball. The goal was not added, but a few minutes later Gayter made a fair catch in a good position, and Farmer steered the ball over the bar. NEITHER GRAMMAR NOR HEADACHE.

Commonly it is women who suffer from headache— not men.

" No, Sir," exclaimed a hardy old friend of the writer's, an individual who knew as little of grammar as of illness, " No, Sir ; I don't: .know nothing about nerves. My head never aches no more than a paving stone."

Yet there are others. Up to the age of thirty I used to have spells of headache which nearly drove me blind and wild. What caused them? Lt us read what Joseph Wood has to say :

" From boyhood I have suffered severely from head ache. All food lay heavy on my stomach, and some- times my legs pained me so I could hardly walk. I was not laid up, but always dreadfully weak, and never really well. " I took tonics and liver mixtures, but was none the better for them. About two years ago I was advised to use Mother Seigel's Syrup, and in a short time it made a new man of me. I am now healthy and strong."— Joseph Wood, Birchmoor, near Tamworth, September 7th, 1899. No more indigestion, no more liver trouble, no more poisonous acids in the blood, no more headache—that is the philosophy of Mr. Wood's recovery. Both men and women should make a note of it.

" In 1887, the first Jubilee year," says another man "my health broke down ; so there was no Jubilee feel- ing for me. I couldn't eat ; the least food gave me great pain at the chest and stomach. Even a drink of water would start the pain ;it made me afraid to eat. For eighteen months I was unable to do a stroke of work. "A I sat in a chair the perspiration would run off me for very weakness ; my flesh wasted like snow under a warm sun. After two years of this I began taking Mother Seigel's Syrup, and in a brief time it cured me completely."—Andrew Shipman, KLIMCOLI, Est Bridg- ford, Notts, S-ptember 15th, 1893.

STOCKTON HEATH.

A Licensing Application Refused.

The Daresbury adjourned fecensing Sessions sere held on Tuesday morning. The magistrates present were Messrs J C Parr (in the chair), John White, H Neild, William Long and Hervey Talbot. Mr. Bon made an applicition on behalf of Mr F A Caress, grocer, of 1, Old London-road, Stockton Heath, for an oat-door beer licence. He said that Mr Caress had already a licence for wines and spirits, and he now asked for an out-door licence to enable him to sell bot- tled beer. He would on no account sell beer in jugs. At Stockton Heath during the last two year;,. 210 houses had been built and the plans for 80 more had already been passed to be built in the immediate neighbourheo 1 of the shop. Therefore trade was more brisk. His client had informed him that he wanted the licence merely to oblige his customers, who, by the way, since they had known that he was to apply for the licence, had placed orders for bottled beer and stout to the extent of 120 deze:s. By granting the licence the Bench would not be increasing the ' number of licences in the village, for his client had already a small licence and it was simply giving him an additional suppll Si.iperintendent Pearson said he hid received irrtruc- done from the Chief Constable of Cheshire to oppose the granting of the licence,there being already sufficient facilities for drinking purposi s. Triers were the Saracen's Head, Red Lion, and the Mulberry Tree, in addition to an off-licence. Mr. Steel, solicitor, also opposed the application, hav- ing baen instructed to de so by several residents and occupiers in the district. The application was supported by Mr.Seddon,Warbur- , ton-street, and Mr. Howarth. Gaskell-street, who said they did not like to send children to the public-house. Mr. Steel contended that another off-licence was un- neeeesary in the neighbourhood. and, as it had been pointed out, there were already four drinking establish- ments. He need not explain the increased dangers attached to the granting of another licence. An objec- tion was that a woman might go to the shop and order the bottled beer as groceries. The shop was also very near to the schools and church. .

Mr. Browne explained that children would not be sent for the bottled beer. It would be sold in dozens. The shop was closed at nine every evening except Saturday, when it was closed at ten. It was also closed on Sun- days. The magistrates retired to consider their decision and on their return tte Chairman said that they bad decided not to accede to the application.

ASSOCIATION FOOTBALL.

Combination. Warrington v Wrexham.

This match was played at Arpley on Saturday after- noon. Originally the match was arranged to be played at Wrexham, but with the Rugby club away from their native heath the chances favoured a good gate. This was Warrington's first appearance in a combination match, and with several capable men men having been signed on a good team was placed in the field. Wrex- ham had their best eleven doing duty, the side including four internationals. Me weather was favourable and the attendance was very satisfactory. Warrington kicked off, and a forward movement by Pierpoint was checked. Wrexham raced away, and after two minutes play Aspinall was beaten. Warrington attacked strongly, and the visitors' custodian had t 3 handle from Riley and Broster. A momentary attack was checked by Cook, but Wrexham returned, and Jones placed the ball through the net but was ruled offside. Smart work on the home left gained ground, but Murray's shot was easily accounted for. Warrington goal had a narrow escape, Aspinall just saving in time. Both teams attacked in turn, but a stubborn defence was offered. Wrexham Tvere penalised, 1 and although Sanders sent the batl into the net the point was disallowed for off-side. A spell of even play followed, but eventually Foster and Murray broke away. The former centred well, but Pierpoint missed an easy chance of equalising. Aspinall was applauded for brilliant saves during a hot; attack. The home backs defended well and cleared their lines. The visitors proved too smart, arld for a second time the ball was sent through the Warrington goal. Thompson was injured and had to retire. Half-time—Wrexham, three goals; Warrington, nil. Rosuming, it was seen that Thompson was unable to take his place, and his absence in a great measure disorganised the play. Wrexham were chiefly the attacking force, but many of their movements were negatived by off-side play. Eventually the Welshmen asserted their superiority, and as the outcome of some clever work the score was aug- mented hy'a goal, the final score being Wrexham, four goals ; Warrington, nil.

Earlestown v. Haydock.

These old rivals engaged in a friendly bout on Satur- day on the Haydock ground. There was a large number of spectators, but as there was nothing at stak very little interest was taken in the game. Earlestown had a good team up, and tried another centre forward in Walker, whilst Haydock were also strongly represented. Haydock were unable to prevail against the Earlestown players, who consideradly ovlrplayed their opponents. The teams meet in the course of a week or so in the, qualifying round of the English Cup Competition, but the match on Saturday could not be considered any criterion of the respective merit of the teams. The game ended in favour of Earlestown by two goals to one.

Warrington Markets.

At the Warrington—Corn Market on Wednesday, new wheat fetched 33 81 to 31 10 (701 b to nthe bushel); new oats, 2s 41; old oats, 2s lid; flour (best seconds) per 2401 b., 2.1 s ; bran, lid per 201 b. àt the Provision Market prices were as follows : Fresh butter, is 31 and is 41 Der lb; cheese, 61, 7d and 8d per lb ; fresh eggs, 8 and 9 for is; fowls, 4s to 5s 61 pr couple ; English beef, 6id to 10d, mutton 7d to 101 ; lamb, 01 to lid ; veal, 7d to 10d; pork, 6d to Bid.
