Drowned in the Mersey.

IMr S Brighouse conducted an inquiry in the Parade Room at the Police Station on Saturday morning relative to the death of Thomas Frederick William Ue.her, who was picked out of the Mersey on the Thursday previous.— Ellen Usher, widow of Thomas Usher, printer, stated that the deceased was her sou and was 33 years of age. He was a forge labourer and lived at 33, Dakin'e-yard. During the last few months he had been in very poor health and in consequence seemed very low spirited. She had never hoard him threaten to take away his life. Detective Meacock stated that on Saturday last at ten minutes to eleven, he was going over Warrington Bridge when he spoke to the deceased. Thomas Lawson, waterman, residing at 17, Mil- street deposed that he was following his employ- ment at Arpley when he saw the body of the deceased floating in the river. He obtained a boat and secured the body which was afterwards removed to the Seven Stars Inn. Detective Barlow said he examined the body but found no marks of violence. He had no property upon him.—The jury returned a verdict of Found Drowned " and the Coroner informed the deceased's mother that there was no evidence to show that the deceased might have taken away his life. lie might have:got into the water accidentally.

BAND OF HOPE RALLY.

Thursday Night's Meeting in the

Parr Hall.

There was only a meagre attendance at the Parr Hall, last Thursday evening,when a grand united rally in con nec:ion with the Warrington and District Band of Hope Union was held. The chair was occupied by Mr. R. A. Naylor. who was accompanied by Miss Naylor and amongst those present on the platform were the .11, vs. R CatteralL (Rector of Crum ,sail) J Yonge and T Bishop, Councillor L Ellison and Messrs W B Suttle (Levens- hulme), C Pigot, F A Jenkins, J Skehand and others. Letters of apology for non-attendance were received from Councillor John Crosfie!cl, Mr R Garnett, Mr Z Armitage, Dr Reginald Smith, Mr J W Hayward, Mr William Owen, Mr Henry '[hornton,MrJ R Youne,Mr T Birchen, Mr T S Steele, Revs James Davies, F EPowell, and E T Reed, and Mr E Lloyd Jones. The CHAIRMAN said the atteniance was very dis- couraging. He thonght that was the smallee, gathering I he had ever noticed at one of the rallies in connection , with the Band of Hope Union. There might he circurn stances, which to some extent, would account for the Isparseness of the assembly, but neveitheless they could not help expressing a little disappointmeet that ' at present instead of the audiences growing larger as time went on, they had gone very much smaller. However, they had two good speakers there that night, who bad been interested in the work for a great number of 3 ear?, and had been working right heartily not only in the district in which they rsilc.'o, but they had gone up and down the country to make known the glad tidings of that work of temperance. His time had been so fully occupied lately that he had been wondering what he could " knock-off " and he had ; decided to ." ktock-cff " politics. There was more. shadow than substance about them (Laughter.) One good thing was that the Election would soon be over, and there would bl so many tens of thousands of lies less, so that was something to be thankful for. (Laugh- ter.) He thought the work which be could extract the greatest amount of good from and which requited the most assistance was the cause of temperance. So far as he was concerned.politics might go to Jericho. There was a lot of humbug about political parties. When they got a party that would go in for temperance and national righteousness and for a high form of patriotism he would go in for party politics again. Unfortunately temperance people had no party to support them. It might be that sometimes they shou'd get some sturdy fellows coming forward in tete cause of temperance and they would rally round them and endeavour to elevate them to the position of member of Parliament There was more need for temperance than ever there was. Last year we spent £160,000,000 in drink. Un- fortunately drunkenness was increasing in some directions.especially amongst women and he was afraid they had to thank Legislation for that. Through an Act of Parliament passed with very good intentions and mistaken idea many a poor woman had gone down to her grave in disgrace and many a home had been darkened and the lives of little children blighted- The field was white already for the harvest but the labourers were few. (Applause.) The Band of Hope Union Choir, under the able con- ductorship of Mr. Walter Brough then gave a capital rendering of Mendelssolin'a part sang •" the open air," Mr. L. Breugh presiding at the organ. _

Mr. W. B.—Stirree then delivered an address. He said he did not think they should be despondent about the meeting. It was av ry good meeting, but the room was rather too large. (Laughter.) They must not be discouraged but they must buckle tighter the armour, when it was getting a little lore,e, and figh on brave:y for the work which God had given them to do. One of the most terrible foes they bad to meet with was indifference. The indifference in society about the question was more appalling than the oppori- don they had to fight against. 'the speaker went on to make an earnest appeal on behalf of the children. They should not be allowed to tamper with stroog drink but should be trained to do without it. Wite regard o the Children's Bill he thought it would be a grand thing if they could protect their children by the law, and not allow them to be served with drink under the age of 16 years. They were told that this would be interfering with the rights of parents, but bad they not interfered already ? Was there not an act passed forbidding little children to be employed in mines ? Toe Band of Hope Union gave them strength to work for temperance principles and he urged them to get all the good out of the Union that they could as unity was strength. He hoped that meeting would be an impetus to the Band of Hope Workers in Warring,.on and that next time they asked Mr Naylor to take the chair the hall would be packed to the doors. (Applause.) The Rev. R CATTERALL observed that the temperance movement had passed the eteges of opposition and toleration and was now a mighty force for good. In some directions, however, it very popularity bad bred indifferentism. Such a meeting as that was in- tended to rowel earnest hearts in Warrington and to make temperance workers more enthusiastic te- wards the movement. They desired the tem- perance movement to be more identified than ever with churches and chapels. The more they were identified with the work of Christian Associations, the more completely would they accomplish reform. He must confess that having regard to the fact that the Band of Hope movement was a national OOP, and was for the children who were the life of the future Ration, he expected that all the churches and chapels would have been represented at that meet- ing. Be was certain they could not help their church or chapel better or help the cause of the Lord Jesus Christ better than by making the whole question of this reform an object of their most earnest prayers morning noon and night. They most educate more. If they did not educate. educate, educate, they would never accomplished anything. Toe three great questions which faced them were the reclametirn of the intemper- ate, the preventien of intemperance and the promotion of legislation. During the evening the choir creditably rendered a • number of hymns and choruses, and the proceedings , closed with the singing of the " Temperance National Anthem."

Ohmy's Circus,—A Realistic "Scene Act." The verdict of all who have visited the circus this week is that the entertainment continues to be much above the average. A capital programme is sustained by a first class company, which is beaded by the Brothers Da Courcev, who have recently been appearing at the London Hippodrome. They are most versatile acrobats and go through some wonderful feats of strength. The Estrellas in their clever gymnastic act are exceedingly popular, and Miss Swallow gives a dashing display on horseback. Mr. Yelding. in his "scene act" is very successful. Dressed as a soldier he rides round the ring fighting against an imagin- ary enemy and is eventually mortally wounded. So realistically was this perlormance carried out on Monday evening, that a soldier in the audi- ence who has just been invalided home from the front, was overcome and fainted. It appears that he received a bullet wound in the leg at Paardeberg, and also took part in the engagement at Omdurman. Mr Anderson performs evolutions on horseback with great agility, and Irvin's foot- balancing is remarkably clever. By means of Edison's warograph some of the most graphic and up-tc-date animated pictures from the fr3nt are exhibited, the entertainment winding up with a fight between Kruger and John Bull. Whimsical Walker with, his " deo of performing lions" creates endless mirth, and other attractive items constitute an excellent programme.

GENERAL ELECTION.

Meeting of Sir J. Brunner's Supporters at Runcorn.

Enthusiastic Gathering at the

Liberal Club.

Address by Sir J. T. Brunner:

- - On Wednesday evoniog, Mr. S imu-4 Taylor, J.P. , pre- sided over a meeting of the supporters of Sir John Brnaner held at the Liberal Club. Toe chief business of the meeting was the passing of a resolutioa approving of Sir John's candidature. Mr. S. Taylor, who on riinz was received very cor- dially, said ne was very pleased to see so good an atten- dance present. It vies five years since they had met together for a similar purpose. Sir John Brunaer had come to give them an account of his conduct for the last five years. He thought, however, as far as that went there was scarcely any need for they would know all about that from what they had heerd and from what they had read. He was sure that Sir John had acquitted himself to the satie- faction of 'every Liberal in Runcorn. (Applauee), Be (the speaker) knew what Sir Jahn Brunner had done from time to time, but he would reserve it for a future occasion. Hitherto it had been the custom to call a meeting for the whole division at Northwich, but this election, as all present were aware, had been sprung upon them so suddenly, and the Northwich election he found was filed a fortnight on Saturday, and on that account a different course had been adopted. Sir John had already spoken at Northwich and at Winsford and on the present occasion he was going to speak to them —(applause).—and as his train went at &treat half-past nine he would call upon him without saying vary much more. Sir John Brunner was presenting himself to them for their acceptance—(applause) —well really it had never occurred to him to mention the subject. (Laughter.) Indeed, it was the last thing he bad in hi; mind, for he felt that Sir John Brunner was the only persoa with the right to represent them—(loud applause).—and he could not believe it fin- less he experienced it,that there was five per cent. of the working people of Runcorn who would de otherwise than support him. (Loud applaus Sir John Brunner, who was warmly receivei, said the tactics of his opponents at this election were characteris- tically Tory. (Hear, heir.) It was impossible for any man to beat him in that constituency on political lines. (Applause.) The whole of Eegland knew that the North- wice Division was Liberal, and all in Nerthwlch Divi- sion knew Runcorn to be as soundly Radical as any per: of her Majesty's dominions, (eppleuse ) In consequence of that knowledge on the part of his opponents, thee were trying to beat him by throwing dirt. Wear. hear.) They had revived again that oil lie that Brunner, Meal and Co., would not take on men over 30 years of age, and he ask el them (his hearers) to stand up for the character oi their old friend and deny that story, and meet any man who told them that story with the strongest argsments thot they knew hew to apply to it. Re desired at their hands a geode character. (Applause) Toe story was not true Taer was hardly a week in the year from the first danu ar y to the 31st of DecemLer, for years past that Brunner Mond and Co, had not take a on men over 30 years of age. And what was more they had about the steadiest set of men in their employ that there was fro.n one cad of the country to the other. They stayed with the firm until their hair grew gray; they stood by them until they were able te work no longer, and whenever a man left their works to live in retirement,if he wore in neces- sitous circumstances they gave him an old age pension to the day of his death. (Applansee Let the repre len- tative of the Ship Canal tel them the same store. (Laughter and applause). Here was their Chairman so thoroughly in sympathy with him (Sir John), that he took it absolutely for granted that they wished him to return to the House of Commons. (Applause ) Mr. Taylor, had given up a holiday, to fight this election. Mr Edward El Mess, president of the Liberal Association, had postponed his holiday until after the election. Mr Henry Brant, chairman of the Northwich district had done the same. This was the spirit he liked to see and that was the conduct he hoped to see imitated from one end of the division to the other. He was told that there was a geanernee in the room who reported there was a story going about in a works in the Runnorn neighbourhood to the eff,ct that when be was giving £5O a week for the poor people et Runcorn during the coal strike, Brunner Mond awl Co., took 15 percent. off their workmen's wages. (" Sh ame.") C+3,l forgive the man who made light of a pure act of charity. ' Let him tell them that the men at Winnington worked eight hour qhifts—l2 hours hard work a day was too much. (Hear, hear.) When the firm started these eight boar shifts the men were given the same wages as they formerly had for twelve hoots, less ten par cent. That vedeetien wee elnletlY heeic eg. (Applause:) This was the only reduction that had taken place in &Inner Mood's work since it had started. (Applause.) It wes a herd thing thet an honest man standing before his old neighbours to solicit from them the honour of representing them in Parliament should be debarred from the discueeion of the noblest political questions and be obliged to desceed to denying such in- famous lying and dirt throwing as this. (Hear, hear). Voice! It has always been the case. Tney have no other policy. (Laughter) ] He asked them indignantly to resist this sort of thing. (Applause.) There was another accusation that he was a pre-Boor. (Laughter.) He was not a prl-Beer but he was decidedly anti-Tory. (Applause.) New Peo was the L Itin for for, and anti was the Latin far against. A strong settled Government had a great deal to do with this presnerity. The working of the two partiee showed that a Unionist regime was all to the benefit of the working man. golonel It. ,The writ was received on Wednesday morning by the P)stina,ter of Warrington (Mr. J. b. Vines'). OUR NEW SERIAL. We have pleasure in announcing that we have secured for publication in our columns on OCTOBER 6th a splendid sensational story, entitled :- 46 'THE HOUSE BY THE RI VER," BY MRS. C. N. WILLIAMSON. WE HAVE NO HESITATION IN SAYING THAT THE HOUSE BY THE RIVER is one of the most sensational stories ever offered for newspaper publication.. It is a splendid conception of one of the most imaginative writers of the day. From the first chapter to the last the interest of the reader is enthralled. What one de nen& in a serial story that is suppose 3 to be sensational is excite- ment, suspense, mystery. and more or less of horror. Re. WILLIAMS3N supplies all these in clever combination. "She has an effective fashion," save the Bookman, "of hodlng back the climax to the last and supreme moment, and of gratifying her readers with an unexpected thrill just when the nerves are at the higheit tension. Yer, she uses an admirable restraint and never becomes hysterical. Her taste is excellent." THE HOUSE BY THE RIVER. The opening chapters of this story will be published in our issue of OCTOBER STII.

TO-THE ELECTORS OF—TI-IE NORTHWICII DIVISION OF THE COUNTY OF CHESTER. LsNTLiMEN,— At the unanimous request of the Unionist party in the Division, I beg to solicit the honour of your support and suffrages at the forthcoming election. I do so as a strong supporter of her Majesty's Government, and I hold that it is the duty of every citizen to set aside all purely personal considerations and to support the Imperial cause at the present election, whieh is probably the most critical that has occurred in the course of her Majesty's reign. The voice o! Northwich has hitherto unfortunately been given, through its present representative, against the Imperial cause. You have now the opportunity of striking a blow for the Empire. and of strengthening the hands of the Government in permanently settling on a satisfactory basis the various questions to which the war with the Boers has given rise. The main issue before the electors is whether her Majesty's Government having regard to its South African policy both before and since the invasion of British terri- tory, is entitled to the confidence of the country, and whether the hands of Ministers should not be strengthened in effecting a settlement which, consequent upon the annexation of the two Boer Repubics, will secure British supremacy, equal rights for all white men, and just and impartial treatm ,nt for all the native races. Other important problems before the Country are the completion of Mr. Chamberlain's great work, begun with the Australian Commonwealth Act, of bringing the Colonies into closer relation r-ith each other and with the Mother Country, especially tor purposes of Defence, the provision of a thoroughly effi,lent: Army and Navy, a drastic reform of the War Office, and the proper recogni- tion and equipment of the Volunteers. As regards domestic questions. I am in favour of a well thought-out and economical system of Old Age Pensions, of the malatenance in a condition of adequate efficiency of the Voluntary Schools. and of such sound social legis- lation as is exemplified in the Workmen's Compensation Act of the present Government. I trust to have an opportunity of meeting you shortly, and of explaining my views upon these and other political questions at greater length. I am. Gentlemen, Your obedient servant, CHARLES L. SAMSON: Carmona, Iferaal. Manchester, September 22nd. 1900.

In every walk of life we encounter this hankering after the (supposed) unattainable, whilst in many cases the remedy is close at hand if those interested only knew how to avail themselves of it. It affects most businesses, the furnishing trade especially. For instance, a young man meets a maiden fair whose appearance and merits he approves. She, likewise, appreciates his qualities, and quickly an affection springs up between them. How pleasant they think it would be to marry and start housekeeping together. Then the stumbling block presents itself—where is the cash to purchase furniture to come from ? With him, time past, like with most young fellows, "enough for the day has been the evil thereof." He has save I practically nothing so the marriage is postponed indefinitely. Now, this is where PITTS S step in with a timely suggestion. A Warrington Policeman Returns from the Front. P.C. Hurst, a member of the Warriegton B)rough Police Force, who has been on active st rviie at the front with the Grenadier Guards since the commence- ment of the war, has bean invalided home, and arrived in Warrington on Saturday. He has been through many battles including Belmont, Graspan, Magersfontein, and Modder River, and was with his regiment 'when they marched into Thoemlontein, and afterwards into Pretoria. Needless to say he was very warmly received, especialy by his fellaw-policemea. THE CRISIS IN CHINA has caused much alarm. A few years ago it would have affected the comfort of every home In England by increasing the cost of tea. To-day, however, with the delightfully fragrant and refreshing British-grown tea which is being shipped weekly to BROOKE BOND SG CO., LTD., from India and Ceylon, tea-drinkers may regard the crisis with equanimity. Thanks to British capital and industry, India and Ceylon have become the great tea-gardens of the world, and we are no longer dependent on the long eyed race who once held the tea monopoly. Although the capital invested in British tea-production amounts to over £14,000,000, there is no tea more highly prized than BRooKE. BOND'S. It is obtained from estates in the finest 'stage of cultivation, is the choicest pick of each season, and is always exquisite and delicious. And yet the Nimble Ninepence Buys Half-a-Pound, and each packet is full weight without the paper I 30,000 AGENTS SELL IT AT 1/6 PER LB. BROOKE, BOND'S TEA.
